#149. Starbucks sales miss expectations, but its U.S. turnaround is gaining steam

Starbucks on Thursday reported mixed quarterly results as its international cafes underperformed expectations.

However, its U.S. turnaround continues to take hold, as its home market topped expectations during the coffee giant's final quarter of fiscal 2026.

"We enter fiscal 2027 with momentum, a clear set of priorities and higher ambitions for what Starbucks can become," CEO Brian Niccol said in a video shared on the company's website.

Starbucks is ahead of schedule to meet the fiscal 2028 targets laid out at its investor day in January, he said on the company's earnings conference call.

For fiscal 2027, Starbucks is projecting adjusted earnings per share in a range of $3.15 to $3.25, above Wall Street's expectations of earnings per share of $3.14. However, its anticipated revenue decline of 1% is weaker than expected; analysts were forecasting net sales growth of 1.4%.

Starbucks is projecting North American same-store sales growth of 4% for the fiscal year.

The company's earnings and revenue outlook includes an additional week and the effects of the shift in its China business to a joint venture business model. Starbucks is also projecting slight deflation in coffee prices and slowing net new North American cafe openings, which will both weigh on its full-year revenue, according to CFO Cathy Smith.

Here's what the company reported for the quarter ended Sept. 27 compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Starbucks reported fiscal fourth-quarter net income attributable to the company of $677.2 million, or 59 cents per share, up from $133.1 million, or 12 cents per share, a year earlier.

Excluding restructuring and impairment costs and other items, the company reported earnings per share of 80 cents.

Net revenue fell 4% to $9.14 billion, reflecting the joint venture deal in China.

The company's global same-store sales increased 7.2%, fueled by growth in average ticket, and beating Wall Street estimates of 6%, according to StreetAccount. Transactions grew 1.8% in the quarter.

Starbucks' U.S. cafes reported same-store sales growth of 7.7%, topping StreetAccount estimates of a 6.5% increase.

Under Niccol's "Back to Starbucks" strategy, the company has focused on improving service, making its cafes more welcoming and sharpening its marketing. The changes have helped to win back customers.

"Starbucks brand affinity is at near record levels as our menu innovation, marketing and merchandise continue to create moments that drive culture and bring people into our coffee houses," Niccol told analysts.

The company said its North American rollout of its fall menu, which includes the iconic pumpkin spice latte, was the company's strongest ever autumn launch day. And matcha is now a $1 billion drink for Starbucks.

Outside of North America, the company's international comparable sales ticked up 0.9%, as traffic declines offset an increase to average ticket. That growth fell short of analyst estimates of a 3.2% increase, according to StreetAccount.

After its China business transitioned to a joint venture, Starbucks has shifted most of its international business to a licensed model, allowing local operators to take charge. The company is also exploring selling a majority stake in its Japan business, Reuters reported in September.

Starbucks opened 303 net new cafes during the quarter. In September, the company announced it would close about 1% of its North American footprint, or about 250 locations, as part of its turnaround.

By the end of its fiscal year, across the U.S. and Canada, the company also completed more than 1,700 cafe uplifts, meant to make its stores more welcoming to customers. The company has said locations that receive a makeover, with tweaks like increased seating and softer lighting, see higher transactions in the aftermath.

#148. CrowdStrike launches AI kill switch that it says can stop internal agents that go rogue

CrowdStrike is betting that it has a solution to rogue artificial intelligence.

The cybersecurity vendor on Tuesday launched an AI kill switch designed to let security teams stop internal AI agents that go rogue, block them from relaunching and cut off their enterprise network access. The tool is built into CrowdStrike's Falcon Guardian platform, launched in September to secure AI agents. CrowdStrike also announced an integration with Anthropic's Claude Cowork.

"Having the ability to control and turn off, and make a decision and decide, I think that's really important for this next chapter of AI adoption," Chief Business Officer Daniel Bernard told CNBC.

A string of recent high-profile cyber incidents has added urgency to calls for some kind of emergency shutdown mechanism.

The Hugging Face breach orchestrated by rogue OpenAI agents in July was particularly alarming for businesses. Last week, Binance disclosed the largest crypto cyberattack on record and hackers have recently targeted hundreds of refrigerators and freezers at supermarkets in France.

Over the summer, U.S. representatives introduced bipartisan legislation that would require AI companies to build shutdown capabilities into their models.

Critics argue, however, that an emergency stop button is far too complicated to implement across multiple systems, data centers and complex AI models.

Bernard said the kill switch operates like a circuit breaker in systems where customers use its Falcon platform. The process can detect internal agent activity but requires a human to decide to cut the agent off and reinstate access.

"If you kill a process at the root, and we have that level of access at the root … it's very binary," Bernard said. "You either kill the AI and the agent, or you don't, and then you report on that."

The kill switch doesn't delete the agent's code but uses connected AI gateways to cut off access to the AI models that influence decisions, CrowdStrike said.

In the age of AI, businesses are moving quickly to adopt autonomous tools, often without the necessary safeguards and protections.

CrowdStrike believes its new tool will let companies stop AI agent 'misbehavior in its tracks, while leaving room for innovation.

The company also announced plans to acquire AI observability startup Fiddler AI and AI security testing firm Dreadnode.

CrowdStrike and its rivals are rapidly scooping up cybersecurity startups to keep up with increasing security demands. The need for AI security tools has also powered a broader wave of cybersecurity deal-making.

#147. From scooters to shampoo - the people saving hundreds from dumpster diving

Darryl Baker was shocked to see piles of clothes dumped beside the recycling bins outside her local supermarket.

On several trips to the dumpsters she has also found a host of other items, such as "a perfectly decent" Ikea picture frame and children's scooters.

Always on the lookout for ways to save money and appalled by the waste, the thrifty mother-of-two from north London asked supermarket staff if she could take some of it.

Permission granted, "I got my children entire new wardrobes," says Darryl, 39, adding that she's stuff put away for years to come.

During a six-month stint this year, "I have probably saved hundreds of pounds," says the freelance journalist.

Darryl reels off the names of brands she has salvaged - Ralph Lauren, Nike and The North Face, among others. "My mind was blown," she says. "This stuff is just strewn all over the floor."

Darryl Baker takes the clothes for herself and her children, and to sell

In addition to finding clothing for her children and her nephews and nieces, she keeps some for herself. Others she donates or sells, including recently getting Â£30 for a men's Barbour jacket.

Darryl has joined the ranks of so-called dumpster divers â people who rummage through commercial bins for treasures. Motivations include saving money, giving items to charity and disgust at how often perfectly usable items are being consigned to waste - or a combination of all three.

Texan Jeff Ferrell has been rooting around in trash for more than 50 years.

"I still dumpster dive every day by bicycle," says the 72-year old, who lives in Fort Worth, and has written books on the topic. "I still enjoy it as much, or more, than ever."

Jeff considers himself a "bit of Robin Hood mixed with anarchist direct action", only scrounging in more affluent neighbourhoods and giving away many of his finds.

"Almost everything in our home is scrounged, and I never buy, for example, soap or toiletries or medical supplies, or shoes and clothes and tools and garden supplies," he says.

Jeff Ferrell sells some of what he finds, and gives away other items

Jeff looks for discarded metal to sell at a scrapyard which, he says, earns him around $150 (Â£110) every two weeks. His wife sells his other finds, such as "an antique or piece of jewellery", on eBay.

Through savings and selling the more valuable items, he estimates his finds are worth "tens of thousands of dollars a year".

While dumpster diving may have been considered taboo in the past, it's now something many people are proud to do and post about online.

Millie Diamond, a London-based stylist, posts videos on Instagram of her rummaging through bins. But she has a very specific aim - to unearth fabrics and clothing that she can salvage and refashion into new items, some of which she then sells.

Millie Diamond turns dumped textiles and clothes into new outfits

She estimates that one dress she found was worth Â£600.

"I'm a bit of a magpie. I like collecting things off the street or anywhere that there's a freebie. If I see something that catches my eye, then I'll grab it."

Millie, 36, began bin diving after spotting some fabrics discarded by a business that had shut down. "I literally clambered into the bin. It turned out it was loads of vintage clothes."

For Charlotte Bayford in Essex it's all about timing. Her best finds come when students at Essex University head home for the summer and ditch anything they don't want to take with them.

This includes half-filled toiletries. "I haven't bought shampoo for eight years," says the 44-year-old sales assistant. "I'd say that has saved me some money."

Like other dumpster divers, Charlotte gives a lot of her finds to charity. But she has also filled her house with salvaged objects, and sells on some valuable items.

Food, as long as it remains sealed, is fair game too. "I've found tins of Heinz baked beans, perfectly fine. I'm quite happy to eat those," Charlotte says.

Charlotte Bayford gives some of her finds to charity

But is taking things out of bins and keeping them acceptable in the eyes of the law?

Jeff, who is professor emeritus of sociology at Texas Christian University, says many US cities have made the practice illegal, including New York, Cleveland and Wichita.

"Dumpster diving is considered illegal [in such places] because that which is thrown away now belongs to the city... or to the private company that provided trash services," he explains. Any dumpster diver also risks trespass charges.

In Fort Worth, where Jeff lives, items can only be removed from bins if they are on public land, external.

In the UK, Sunjay Versani of Duncan Lewis Solicitors says it depends on the circumstances.

"In many cases involving genuinely discarded goods, prosecution is unlikely," he says.

"But there is no general rule that something is free to take simply because it has been put in a skip or bin. If in doubt, the safest approach is to ask the owner or business for permission."

#145. AMD has been crushed into earnings. Here’s one way to trade it

Advanced Micro Devices has been under significant pressure leading up to earnings after the close. The stock has been caught in the swings of the AI debate all year, but this most recent leg lower was triggered by a wave of disappointing tech earnings.

A record drop in Alphabet last Thursday was followed by weak results from Amazon, as well as AMD's long-standing CPU rival, Intel. Intel guided fourth-quarter revenue well below consensus and expects the PC slowdown to last through at least the first half of next year. That sent Intel's stock down 29% on Friday, its worst day since the 1970s. Caught in the fallout of Intel and the broader tech sector, AMD fell 10.9% that day and slid another 7.5% Monday.

For the past week, AMD has traded on other companies' news. Now its own numbers get a vote.  AMD's stock reactions have been mixed in recent quarters, sensitive to guidance and high investor expectations. It fell 17% after Q4 2025 results, when a beat and a guide above estimates still underwhelmed some analysts. It rose 18.6% after Q1 2026 results, when it guided about 6.5% above expectations. It fell 7% after Q2 2026 results, when a guide above consensus still fell short of the $14 billion some analysts wanted. That may make direction hard to call, but options let investors build a trade that doesn't have to pick a side.

Based on Monday's close, I'm selling the November 4 425/415 put spread and the 510/520 call spread for a credit of roughly $3.65.

AMD's 30-day implied volatility is elevated going into earnings, closing Monday higher than on about 81% of days over the past year. This means sellers are collecting richer premium than usual. To capture that premium while limiting time beyond the earnings announcement, the trade uses the November 4 weeklies. These contracts imply a move of about 9% through Wednesday, in line with AMD's 8.4% average close-to-close move over its last 10 reports. The short strikes sit at the edges of the expected move, with long strikes $10 away to limit capital at risk going into earnings. This isn't a bet that options are mispricing the move or a directional call. It's a neutral play that aims to profit from the elevated premium that tends to come out after the print.

The trade reaches its maximum profit of $365 if AMD closes between $425 and $510 on Wednesday. Breakevens are $421.35 and $513.65, about 9.7% below and 10.0% above Monday's close, just outside the expected move. The maximum loss of $635 comes if AMD closes below $415 or above $520, roughly 11% in either direction. That has happened in 11 of its last 40 reports, and AMD has had moves as large as 19% in its last four, emphasizing the importance of capping the risk with the long wings.

If AMD finishes between $415 and $425, the short put can be assigned, creating an obligation to buy 100 shares per spread at $425. After the credit, the cost basis is $421.35, or roughly 35% below the Oct. 6 closing high. For investors who like AMD's long-term story, that's a way to end up owning the stock at a discount. A finish between $510 and $520 can assign the short call and leave a short stock position. For investors looking to avoid either risk, consider managing the position before Wednesday's close.

#144. Mining giant joins fight to stop killer toad march across Australia

Rio Tinto is ploughing millions of dollars into stopping the poisonous cane toad reaching the Pilbara region of western Australia, where the mining giant has a vast iron ore operation.

The firm, alongside Curtin University in Perth and others, will spend A$28m (Â£14.5m) building a "toad containment zone" to prevent the pest travelling south.

While the cane toad is not a direct threat to Rio Tinto's business, it produces toxins lethal to native wildlife and poses a threat to Pilbara's ecosystem.

Rio Tinto has previously faced criticism for destroying two sacred Aboriginal rock shelters in Pilbara which sat above tonnes of iron ore.

The decision cost the former chief executive his job, provoked a widespread backlash and saw Rio Tinto commit to rebuilding trust with Indigenous communities.

Commenting on the proposed containment zone, Roberta Hunter, a ranger in Australia's Nyangumarta Warrarn Indigenous Protected Area, said: "Cane toads destroy the water and animals.

"When toads come close to our country, we want to be there to stop them because we don't want the country to be destroyed."

The plan is to cut off the toad's water supply by building a barrier across 7,500 sq km (2,895 sq m) of land between the Great Sandy Desert and the Indian Ocean.

In drier months, the cane toad needs water every few days to survive.

If there are no natural water sources, the toad relies on artificial water points normally used for cattle and irrigation.

The funding will be used to stop the toad accessing dams, storage tanks or other ground-level water sources. The five-year project will begin in 2027.

A number of native species are at risk if the toad gets into Pilbara. These include the northern quoll, whose numbers have been decreasing, the goanna lizard and the ghost bat.

The toads were introduced into Australia in 1935 to control beetles within the sugar cane industry.

The northern quoll is at risk from the cane toad in Australia

Since then, they have been on a slow march west across the country's northern landscape, according to the Australian government, travelling between 40 km and 60 km a year.

There are no predators or diseases that control the toads, which defend themselves using poison.

Ben Phillips, a professor at Curtin University, said the toads would reach Pilbara within two decades without action.

He said: "The toad containment zone is a once in a lifetime opportunity to stop one of the world's worst invasive species in its tracks and protect the region's unique wildlife."

#142. Cheaper cars are selling considerably faster than at the beginning of the year

Consumers are concerned about rising fuel prices and affordability — and that could be showing up in how fast the least expensive cars are moving off dealer lots.

From the first quarter to the third, the number of days it took a dealer to sell a vehicle priced between $20,000 and $29,999 dropped 20%, according to data from Edmunds. That pace of selling saw the biggest acceleration of any price segment.

Vehicles priced from $30,000 to $39,999 sold about 18% faster, and the number of days it took to sell the next tier — $40,000 to $49,999 — dropped nearly 12%, according to Edmunds.

All of those price segments also took fewer days to turn than their more expensive counterparts. The lower-priced tiers took under 60 days to move off lots, with the least expensive ones selling the fastest. Every segment of vehicles above $50,000, meanwhile, took more than 60 days to sell.

"If you're a dealer and you have inventory and that price point, you're definitely noticing," said Edmunds Director of Insights Ivan Drury.

Cheaper vehicles may be selling faster, but they're still hard to find. The average listing price for a new vehicle in September was $49,726, according to Cox Automotive.

And, the overall sales numbers for some key segments priced under $40,000 have fallen. This has especially been true for compact cars, which are down roughly 4% since the beginning of 2026, and compact SUVs, down nearly 8% in the same period, according to Cox Automotive.

These different data points seem to be moving in opposite directions. On the one hand, cheaper cars are moving faster off dealer lots. On the other, sales of affordable segments are down.

There are a few reasons for this, according to analysts who follow the industry. One is that there might not be enough lower-priced vehicles on dealer lots in the first place.

"I think there are some supply things going on at that end of the market," said Cox Automotive Deputy Chief Economist Mark Strand. "We have to kind of grade the market on a curve until we see what's going on with fourth-quarter data."

The compact SUV segment, in particular, is suffering from the lack of two popular models.

The Toyota RAV4, which is the most popular SUV in the U.S., has been in short supply at many dealerships this year, as the automaker shifts production to its Kentucky factory, Strand said. The company also has shifted the entire lineup to a hybrid powertrain at a time when hybrids have been in exceptionally high demand.

The other missing SUV is the Ford Escape, which that automaker discontinued to make way for its upcoming midsize EV pickup truck.

The other slow-selling, low-priced segment — compact cars — faces structural inventory challenges, said AutoForecast Solutions Vice President of Global Forecasting Sam Fiorani.

"We've seen the disappearance of affordable, compact new vehicles over the last decade," he said.

Detroit automakers swapped out their lineups of compact coupes and sedans in favor of small crossover SUVs, which tend to carry slightly higher sticker prices than comparably sized passenger cars.

Currently, there is not a single compact passenger sedan for sale by an American manufacturer. Most on the market are Japanese or Korean, with a few German models, such as the Volkswagen Jetta and the Golf hatchback.

"You have all these brands that have just abandoned the marketplace," Fiorani said.

The smallest American passenger cars are the Ford Mustang, General Motors' Chevrolet Corvette, and the midsize Tesla Model 3 sedan, according to AutoForecast Solutions. The firm classifies both the Mustang and the Corvette as sports cars, and as such they occupy a different niche than compact sedans like the Honda Civic or Toyota Corolla.

The Mustang has a starting price of $33,490, the Model 3 starts at $34,990 and the Chevrolet Corvette starts at $71,300. Cars like the Civic, Corolla and Jetta, meanwhile, all start below $25,000.

"Compact vehicle buyers are very price-sensitive," Fiorani said. "And so when the price of gas goes up, that eats into their car payment. That means that they can't afford a $500 or $600 a month car payment, and they have to move into the used market."

Indeed, the wholesale used prices of compact cars have risen and are up more than almost any other segment, according to Cox Automotive. Wholesale prices — the prices vehicles sell for at large auctions, like Cox Automotive's Manheim auction — are indicators for prices overall in the fragmented used-car market.

Along with the rise in prices, auto finance stats show that a historically high number of buyers are stretching to afford cars.

Roughly one in four buyers financing their purchases are getting 84-month loans — a record. Nearly 3 in 10 trade-ins toward new-vehicle purchases carried negative equity in the third quarter, meaning the cars being traded in were worth less than the outstanding balances of the loans on them. Shoppers are often rolling these negative balances into new loans, which pushes up the new payment cost.

Automakers have also been more tightly aligning production with demand to avoid discounting vehicles. Incentives on average are about 6.6% of the average transaction price as of October, according to Cox Automotive. Leasing offers a way to get a car with a lower payment, but leasing rates are also down.

Vehicle trade-in data suggests that many buyers who are snapping up those fast-selling cheaper vehicles are either first-time buyers or are purchasing supplemental vehicles, Edmunds' Drury said.

The subcompact SUV segment is one lower-priced group that has seen sales rise in 2026. That is a segment that attracts a lot of first-time buyers. But this is still a relatively small share of the total car market, Fiorani said.

"It's not the mainstream vehicle that Americans want," he said. "It is the vehicle that entry-level buyers need to get to work, to get to school. ... It's people who don't have families yet."

Repeat buyers are less likely to want to step down into a compact car or SUV, Drury said.

"The moment you get into that other vehicle and it's a little bit smaller, it's not as comfortable, it doesn't fit all your stuff, all your kid's equipment for soccer practice, you're done," he said. "You're not giving up 360 cameras, ventilated seats, heated steering wheels, these niceties that you've become accustomed to. Automakers know that too. Dealers know that."

The truly cash-strapped are either snapping up the relatively few low-priced cars on dealer lots, moving to the used market, or holding on to their cars longer.

"The people who are trying to buy the smaller vehicles are really hurt by the way the economy is at the moment," Fiorani said. "So those people are trying to move into used vehicles or trying to maintain their current vehicle as long as they can. Automakers should be prepared for a softening in the marketplace."

#140. Asda hires ex-Morrisons boss in bid to grow firm

Asda headquarters are based in Leeds city centre

Asda has appointed a former Morrisons boss as chief executive to help lead revival efforts at the supermarket chain.

Asda, which is based in Leeds, said David Potts would take on the role with immediate effect, while current boss Allan Leighton would become non-executive chairman, two years after returning to lead the supermarket chain.

Potts led Morrisons from its Bradford headquarters for eight years until 2023, overseeing a turnaround during which the supermarket chain was bought by US private equity firm Clayton, Dubilier & Rice.

Potts said Asda had "made good progress but significant opportunities remain" to reinvigorate sales, with a focus on keeping prices low for shoppers.

"By making improvements to the shopping trip every week, which customers can experience and feel in their pockets, we will revitalise the business and bring benefits for all our stakeholders, including our 116,296 very hard-working colleagues," he said.

Before joining Morrisons, Potts was chief executive of Tesco's Irish business, its UK arm and its Asian operation.

David Potts led Morrisons for eight years until 2023

According to Asda, Leighton had planned to step aside from the day-to-day running of the group, after he returned in 2024, more than two decades after serving as chief executive.

"Over the last two years, we have stabilised the business and returned it to some growth," said Leighton.

"David has an outstanding record in our industry, his experience is exactly what we now require for the next stage of our transformation and beyond and it will be great to work with him."

Last year, issues with an IT upgrade hit product availability at the supermarket and dented sales, impacting its bottom line.

But in August, it reported sales had returned to growth for the current quarter for the first time in two years.

#139. Drivers may get compensation offers before car finance saga ends

Drivers may be offered compensation from lenders ahead of the next legal battle in the car finance mis-selling saga.

Regulator the Financial Conduct Authority (FCA) said it would welcome lenders making settlement offers as long as customers were not given a worse deal than under its proposed central scheme.

That scheme is facing a legal challenge, to be heard in February, with resulting compensation unlikely to be paid until late next year.

But Barclays has confirmed it will make early compensation offers from now, with others expected to follow suit.

The FCA said it would still only expect a minority of claimants to be made early offers, with most having to wait for the outcome of next year's court case.

Stephen Braviner Roman, the FCA's general counsel and co-lead on the motor finance project, said: "Fair settlement offers could result in some people getting money sooner. It's clear they want the choice.

"Anyone who wants to wait can, and there's no obligation to agree to the offer. We'll closely monitor firms making offers and expect them to be clear what accepting an offer means."

The vast majority of new cars, and many second-hand ones, are bought with finance agreements. Customers pay an initial deposit to secure the vehicle, then a monthly fee with interest.

But millions of drivers who took out a car loan between April 2007 and November 2024 will be entitled to compensation after the loans were mis-sold.

They mostly related to when car dealers received commission from lenders, based on the interest rate charged to the customer. These were known as discretionary commission arrangements (DCAs) and customers were often not told about them.

Average compensation payouts of about Â£829 are expected to be made related to about 12 million car loans - about 37% of the total number during the period.

Car finance compensation payments delayed until next year

Compensation will cost the industry up to Â£9.1bn, including administrative costs. Individual payouts will vary, depending on the degree of harm each driver faced.

With huge compensation payouts inevitable, some lenders have already set aside millions of pounds to cover the cost.

Barclays has decided to start making compensation payments early, potentially to start clearing the cost off its balance sheet. It pointed out that claimants had already faced "a considerable wait".

"By making these early settlement offers, we are giving some customers the option of receiving redress sooner, rather than waiting for the legal challenges and any subsequent FCA action to conclude," said a Barclays Partner Finance spokesperson.

"Each offer will be calculated using the FCA's redress scheme methodology. We will clearly explain how it has been calculated and what accepting a full and final settlement means, so customers can decide whether accepting the offer is right for them."

The lender said claimants would start to be contacted directly "in the coming weeks".

But it stressed not all customers whose complaints were acknowledged on or before 1 October 2026 would receive an early settlement offer.

The FCA has published a factsheet to help drivers, external work out whether they should accept early settlements from lenders.

It will include a list of lenders who are making early offers, to ensure claimants with other lenders are not scammed.

The regulator said lenders will need to be clear with people about what accepting any offer means. This includes:

Being able to keep any payment, even if the final settlement under the central scheme after the legal challenges proved to be smaller

Claimants would not be able to claim more later if the outcome of the legal challenge meant they would have been paid more

Accepting an offer takes you out of the scheme and stops any further input from the financial ombudsman

The Finance and Leasing Association, which represents lenders, said: "We want to see consumers receive the fairest outcome in as timely a way as possible and recognise that, in some cases, that may point to early settlement, while in others it may be appropriate to await greater legal certainty."

But Kevin Durkin, solicitor advocate and director at HD Law, who represented Marcus Johnson who won his case in the Supreme Court, said he had "real reservations" that fair compensation would be offered.

"These are only heightened by reports that just a small number of claimants will receive what could be lowball offers. I fear this is crumbs from the table, for some customers who are highly likely to be owed more, while the vast majority will receive no offer at all," he said.

The main proposed FCA scheme remains partially suspended, pending legal challenges in February.

Consumer group Consumer Voice said the proposed compensation scheme left "too many people short-changed". Three lenders - Volkswagen Financial Services, Mercedes Benz Financial Services, and Credit Agricole Auto Finance - are also challenging the scheme.

The UK's Upper Tribunal has agreed to hear legal challenges to the scheme, with a hearing scheduled from 16 February to 3 March.

Anyone who is concerned about their car finance deal and has not yet complained can still complain directly to their lender. There is information on the FCA website on how to complain for free, external.

#138. Jim Cramer sees signs of life in tech stocks — but not from the usual winners

CNBC's Jim Cramer said Tuesday that a new group of technology stocks may be emerging as market leaders.

While heavyweights such as Nvidia, Microsoft, Alphabet and Meta have struggled to sustain rallies in recent weeks, other companies are showing signs of strength. That was evident during Tuesday's session as gains from Broadcom and Micron supported the major averages — a sign that investors may be shifting toward a different set of tech winners.

"Tech's trying to make a stand here. It's just not the tech that's led us for the last few years," the "Mad Money" host said.

Cramer pointed to chipmaker Advanced Micro Devices and networking company Arista Networks — both of which reported strong quarterly results after the bell Tuesday — as examples of technology companies that could help lead the sector's recovery. He also highlighted Oracle, whose shares jumped nearly 6% in Tuesday's session.

"I want to believe that non-magnificent tech can lead us," he said. "But I also know that there are concerns we must be aware of before we get too cocky about one session's set of positives."

One concern is growing opposition to data center development, as local communities push back due to fears about rising electricity costs and the facilities' water consumption. He said smaller developers such as Iren, Nebius and CoreWeave could be particularly vulnerable if projects are canceled or forced to relocate, while larger companies like Amazon have more resources to secure alternative locations.

The ongoing war with Iran is another source of uncertainty. Oil prices turned lower Tuesday on hopes there could be progress in U.S.-Iran negotiations. But Cramer cautioned that Wall Street has repeatedly underestimated the risk of further escalation, making it difficult to declare the conflict no longer a threat to markets.

He stopped short of saying that the sector had reached a lasting bottom. After months of failed rebounds, Cramer said he "would like to wait for the crescendo before I commit too much money back into tech."

#137. Graduates are managing AI - but are they learning much else?

Managing AI agents is a big part of Lotte Underwood's job

Recent university graduate Lotte Underwood is a public relations executive with a twist - she spends more time using AI than actually doing PR.

Underwood, 25, joined London-based PR agency Fight or Flight in November last year, in the new role of "AI associate" after completing a degree and then a masters in marketing.

She's part of a recently established, dedicated AI team that has built around 30 AI agents. Specific tasks include industry research, client reporting, media interview preparation, helping clients create clearer briefs, and image generation.

A big part of Underwood's job is to manage those agents, check their outputs and feed those to the team members who are dealing directly with clients and the media.

Another key responsibility of hers is to monitor how clients appear in the responses from AI tools like ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini and others.

"A huge part is being in client meetings and calls to work out what is actually going to be most helpful from these agents," says Underwood.

AI is seen as one of the main factors behind graduate job cuts, but companies are also creating dedicated AI-specific graduate roles like Underwood's.

Dillan Patel, for example, joined engineering software company Stride 18 months ago as an intern. A business management graduate, he was put to work developing an internal chatbot designed to capture the knowledge of a senior staff member transitioning into retirement.

Patel, 24, is now an AI automation analyst, tasked with developing further internal agents to provide specific information to individual teams, while also identifying which manual or repetitive tasks across the company could be done by AI.

"My role focuses on finding practical ways to use technology, automation, data and AI to improve how we operate," says Coventry-based Patel.

Debra Maxwell is looking for "AI disruptors"

Such AI savvy graduates are also in demand from companies like ArvatoConnect, an outsourced contact centre company.

CEO Debra Maxwell says the company's usual graduate intake is on hold this year, but she plans to resume it next year with a bigger focus on AI.

"We would be looking for more AI-disruptor type roles, where you can challenge us as an organisation to think more holistically about where AI can be applied and where we could grow our business from," says London-based Maxwell.

"We see our graduates as not filling administrative roles, but actually adding value to the organisation in a different way."

Maxwell adds: "For example, one of our recent graduates is using AI to do deep dives on specific markets, and provide more information for when we're bidding [for new contracts]. It's basically giving him a more rounded role."

If AI can make a graduate job bigger and more integral to the organisation, Maxwell argues that companies can create a more loyal, engaged workforce that is able to contribute more meaningfully, much earlier.

Research from consultancy PwC found that some graduate roles are assuming more responsibilities because of AI, as it allows companies to demand more senior abilities from junior hires.

For the consultancy's 2026 AI Jobs Barometer, external, it analysed 2.4 million entry-level jobs in the US. It found that the entry-level roles most influenced by AI are now seven times more likely to require traditionally senior-level skills. These include motivational leadership, strategic decision making and team building.

Those entry-level roles with higher expectations have in fact grown 35% since 2019, PwC found, while other entry-level roles actually shrank by 10%.

Cian Clinton uses around ten AI agents for work tasks

Cian Clinton, who joined consultancy Valliance last September after graduating from a Bachelor of Science in 2024, says the incorporation of AI into his role makes him feel less like a graduate, and more like an equal team member. He uses around 10 AI agents daily for different tasks.

"Your ideas and your creativity are a lot more valued because you actually get to implement those firsthand, and the responsibility you take on is a lot greater because of that," says London-based Clinton, 23.

"I am now taking on responsibilities which would usually take me four or five years to get to."

His career progression, he believes, will now be faster as a result.

"AI is killing the 'pyramid' approachâ¦progression is no longer based on your knowledge bank. Now it's more based on your critical thinking and understanding of the processes," says Clinton.

There's no doubt Underwood, Patel and Clinton are developing valuable AI knowledge which can help them across their careers.

But with their time at work so focused on AI, does it matter how much - or how little - time they're spending learning about the professional field they're supposed to be operating in?

Underwood says she'd like to still pick up the traditional PR skills her role isn't currently focused on, but is confident of her future in the agency's growing AI team.

"There's certain things that I haven't had the chance to do yet, like directly pitching to the media. I absolutely plan to do this," she says.

Graduates becoming too AI-centric is an issue for Simon Pratt, chief operating officer at Portman Finance Group. He says being able to use AI effectively is important, but the company's two-year graduate programme is fully centred on "learning the trade".

"Our graduates rotate across four areas at six-month intervals, working directly alongside senior relationship managers to learn their trade," Northampton-based Pratt explains.

"They'll learn all of our financial products, how to draft credit papers for submission to lenders, and how to build meaningful relationships with our customers. They'll progress to running transactions from start to finish."

Claire Reed, PwC UK's chief technology and innovation officer, believes graduate roles that are too AI heavy won't necessarily provide graduates long-term career progression.

"There will be no graduate job now that doesn't require a baseline of AI literacy," says Glasgow-based Reed.

"But AI skills on their own are not going to help you in your career. You've got to couple that with that sector knowledge of what that client actually wants."

Back in Coventry, Dillan Patel is confident his AI work will continue to be valuable, and he wants to keep building on it.

"I don't think I'd want to explore different positions within the business. Through my involvement in AI-related projects, I already work closely with different areas of the business and I'm gaining valuable knowledge and experience along the way," he says.

#136. Strand bookstore to pay nearly $1.5 million over Covid loan tied to Sen. Wyden's wife

The Strand, the iconic New York City bookstore, agreed to pay nearly $1.5 million in a civil settlement for accepting a Covid government loan that was off-limits because the store is owned by the wife of Oregon Democratic Sen. Ron Wyden, according to the U.S. Attorney's Office in Washington, D.C.

The Strand applied for the $932,350 loan in February 2021 from the Paycheck Protection Program administered by the Small Business Administration, U.S. Attorney Jeanine Pirro's office said Tuesday.

Store owner Nancy Bass Wyden certified on the loan application that "the spouse of a Member of Congress does not directly or indirectly hold a controlling interest in the applicant," Pirro's office said.

"Ms. Wyden's certification was knowingly and materially false and Strand was not eligible for the second draw loan it received," the office said.

The Strand will pay $1,482,560 to settle the case. There was no determination of liability by the store, Pirro's office said.

The Strand, in a statement, said the store made an "unintentional" mistake in applying for the loan, and said it was unaware at the time the funds were barred to businesses owned by the spouse of a member of Congress.

Bass Wyden's husband, who has served in the Senate for more than 30 years, is a staunch critic of President Donald Trump, who appointed Pirro to the post of top prosecutor in the District of Columbia.

Bass Wyden is the granddaughter of Ben Bass, founder of the Strand, whose flagship store is in Greenwich Village.

Congress authorized the forgivable PPP loans when it passed the Coronavirus Aid, Relief, and Economic Security Act in March 2020.

In December 2020, Congress provided additional funding for the PPP loan program with separate legislation, which "included a prohibition against businesses obtaining second draw PPP funds if a member of Congress or their spouse directly or indirectly held a controlling interest in the PPP loan applicant," Pirro's office said.

In a statement Tuesday, Pirro said, "The Paycheck Protection Program was created to save American jobs, not to enrich those who aren't eligible for taxpayer-funded relief, especially the wife of a U.S. Senator."

Her office noted that the False Claims Act, which the Strand was alleged to have violated, "allows the government to establish liability based on reckless disregard and permits the government to recover more than its actual losses," which is what occurred in this case.

The civil settlement resolved claims brought under a so-called qui tam lawsuit, which allows private whistleblowers to sue on behalf of the U.S. government and receive a portion of any recovery.

The qui tam action in this case was filed by a corporate entity, Blockquote Inc., in U.S. District Court in Washington. Blockquote, whose owners were not disclosed by Pirro's office, will receive a total share of about $148,256 in connection with the settlement.

The docket for Blockquote's lawsuit is sealed from public view.

The Strand, in a statement after CNBC requested comment from the store and Bass Wyden, said the restriction that the store violated was passed by Congress between the first and second rounds of the PPP.

"Unfortunately, the bookstore's accountant and the bank administering the loan were not aware of this change when the second-draw application was processed," the Strand said.

"We stand by the facts: the circumstances arose during an unprecedented and rapidly changing public health emergency, the mistake was unintentional," the store said. "We hope this matter will be viewed objectively and in its proper context, with the understanding that the application of justice should always be fair, impartial, and free from political considerations," the store said.

A spokesman for Sen. Wyden's office said the senator and his wife do not discuss her business, and that he had no involvement in the loan or settlement.

#135. Canva announces major job cuts after 'our hardest year yet'

Canva co-founder Melanie Perkins says this year has been "our hardest" yet

Canva, one of Australia's best known start-ups, has announced a shake up of the business that will affect 22% of its workers, including job cuts.

In what was "without doubt the hardest moment in our thirteen-year history" co-founder and CEO Melanie Perkins said 1,271 people would either lose their jobs, be asked to relocate or change roles.

The firm's value has reportedly dropped in the past year over concerns about the impact of artificial intelligence (AI) on its business as well as the cost of incorporating the technology into its platforms.

It had been "our hardest year yet, and as we shared earlier this year, our growth has slowed for the first time in a decade," Perkins wrote to staff.

The "incredibly hard decision" meant "significant changes," Perkins said.

"For some people, this will mean changes to their roles or being asked to relocate to centralised hubs. Sadly, it will also mean saying goodbye to teammates and friends who have helped build Canva into what it is today."

The company, which has staff in 37 countries, said Sydney would become the "primary home" for more of its product teams and that as of next year hybrid workers would be expected to spend two to three days in the office.

Earlier this year two of its backers, Blackbird Ventures and Airtree, reportedly downgraded its valuation from A$60bn ($41.8bn; Â£31.1bn) at the end of last year to about A$49bn.

Canva also reportedly cut its own internal valuation amid soaring AI expenses related to the roll out of new products.

"New technology is transforming what people can create, how they create it, and what they expect from the products they use," Perkins said in her note on Wednesday.

Perkins, who left university at 19 to found her first venture, launched Canva in 2013.

The company offers a variety of templates and tools to help users without technical skills or a design background to create a range of professional-looking graphics and presentations, from wedding invitations to business pitch decks.

#134. Automakers like Rivian and Ford are ‘democratizing 3D printing’ to reduce costs and increase speed

DETROIT — Automakers including Ford Motor and Rivian Automotive are leaning into a new type of manufacturing line: 3D printing.

Manufacturers say it's helping them reduce costs and increase speed. The technology has been around for decades, but it's becoming more commoditized in the automotive industry, with 3D printers used to create everything from vehicle parts and accessories to tools for assembly plants and testing units for research and development labs.

"At this point, we don't have a choice but to scale up and begin using it through all factions of Rivian," Jonathan Dankenbring, Rivian senior manager of prototype manufacturing, told CNBC during a tour of the automaker's expansive 3D-printing operations in suburban Detroit. "We're going to be democratizing 3D printing for the entire company."

Also known as additive manufacturing, 3D printing can create three-dimensional objects from digital designs by dispensing material layer by layer, much like a traditional printer does with ink on paper. Instead of ink, the machines use a wide variety of materials such as plastics, composites, ceramics and even metals.

Printers can range from hundreds of dollars for hobby-like devices to hundreds of thousands of dollars for specialty equipment, depending on how big the product being produced is and the material it needs. They can produce individual pieces or hundreds of smaller parts in a batch using powders and resins.

Rivian said it has spent more than $5 million on 3D-printing machines, with the cost of materials for the operations growing to about $1.7 million annually.

Dankenbring said despite the costs, the machines typically pay for themselves within a year. He said the technology helps lower supplier tooling costs, streamline logistics expenses and cut production times for parts from weeks to days, if not hours.

"It's well in the millions [for savings], but the main thing is speed for Rivian," Dankenbring said.

The all-electric vehicle company's latest expansion of 3D printing includes adding new machines that can build flexible silicone parts as well as installing a bay of machines at its plant in Normal, Illinois, for employees to print parts on demand.

"3D prints should be readily available for every engineer so that they can make a part and get it in their hands as soon as possible," Dankenbring said.

Rivian has also used the technology for electric-motor models for fluid testing, creating screw guides for tooling and stamping press inserts and some production parts, including trim pieces to fill gaps in some early builds.

There's a similar push underway at larger, more-established automakers like Ford and its crosstown rival General Motors, which have used 3D printing for many years, including in their massive assembly plants.

Instead of having a bay of 3D-printing machines like at Rivian, Ford has departments for additive manufacturing at many of its plants to produce and distribute needed parts. That includes a new vending machine for 3D-printed parts for production of F-150 pickup trucks near its Dearborn, Michigan, headquarters.

"By putting the vending machine up, all the team leaders have access," said Gregory Foresi, a Ford mechanical engineer who ran Ford's 3D-printing lab at the plant from 2024 until last month. "We're able to keep that thing stocked rather than have a bunch of parts laying around in the lab. So that's been pretty huge."

Foresi used roughly two dozen machines to print off thousands of tools, some of which have been designed by employees, such as torque bit sockets, hood jackers and hand tools for smoothing adhesive.

Employees can use an app to request that specific auto parts or tools be printed. Ford's 3D-printing teams help develop them or determine what to produce next, as the machining time to build different parts can range from hours to days, if not weeks.

Foresi said creating the tools with 3D printing has allowed the automaker to uniquely design them when needed, get them faster and, in many cases, receive them at a lower cost.

Ford also has used 3D-printed parts to help keep production running. For example, Foresi said a large machining wheel was needed to maintain assembly line production and the company was able to 3D print the part until a permanent replacement from a supplier was available.

"They didn't perform as well, and it's not really a replacement for the ones that we were purchasing, but they kept the plant running," Foresi said.

Ford said it expects to have 3D labs deployed at more than 23 plants by the end of this year. Its current 3D-printing program has more than 200 machines, and there are additional printers outside the official program, the company said.

GM has used 3D-printed parts for some of its low-volume products in addition to research and development and inside plants, including recently expanding its use of industrial Stratasys printers that can cost hundreds of thousands of dollars based on use case, training and certification.

The Detroit automaker uses 135 components that are 3D-printed for its bespoke Cadillac Celestiq as well as other components for its performance Cadillac Blackwing sedans, including a unique medallion on the manual shifter knob.

Fadi Abro, senior global director of automotive and mobility at 3D-printing company Stratasys, said GM has been a great example of how companies can "standardize and then scale" 3D printing.

"The material properties improved so significantly that the use cases improved," he said, noting the company works with virtually every major automaker. "From a product perspective, we have to get the technology to become faster and more accessible, in an economic sense."

Correction: This story has been updated to reflect that Jonathan Dankenbring is Rivian's senior manager of prototype manufacturing. A previous version of this article misidentified him.

#133. Binance says fake job interviews used as part of $6.3bn crypto hack

Hackers who stole a record $6.3bn (Â£4.7bn) from Binance used fake job interviews and a compromised internal AI tool, the firm has revealed.

Initial findings published by the company, which operates the world's largest cryptocurrency exchange, said hackers gained access to its systems through "sophisticated, multi-stage campaign".

It said they used a range of techniques such as malicious software downloads to compromise the computers of Binance engineers and gain control over tools to drain its wallet of billions of dollars worth of crypto.

Binance called it a "serious and highly sophisticated security incident" and apologised, saying "this should not have happened".

In a post on X, external, Binance said hackers targeted its engineers using social engineering techniques.

These see hackers use approaches such as phishing to trick people into parting with sensitive or personal details, that can then be used to try and access their accounts.

Binance said as part of the hack, one of its engineers was contacted through a legitimate job-hunting platform and invited to bogus job interviews with fake company representatives.

It said the perpetrators used "more advanced and deceptive" methods to build trust with him, such as sharing social media accounts that contained personal images.

The engineer in question was instructed to download malware masquerading as an "assignment" during a second interview, that deployed malicious code with "obfuscation techniques" evading detection.

Binance said hackers then leveraged the engineer's compromised computer and a compromised internal AI tool in October to gain control over two other engineers' laptops, and execute the next steps of the attack.

The incident led Binance to suspend withdrawals for customers globally, meaning users could not get their cryptocurrency out of the platform until the service was restored on Sunday.

Customers withdrew billions of dollars worth of assets after it reopened.

"We apologise for the concern, disruption and uncertainty it caused our users, partners and the wider industry," it said.

The company added it had taken a range of steps following the incident, including working with law enforcement to trace and freeze affected assets.

It said it had also set aside more than 100% of the funds needed to cover the loss, noting 98% of the token assets impacted had been "replenished" as of Saturday.

The record $6.3bn (Â£4.7bn) attack is thought to be the largest crypto hack to date.

What we know about the $6.3bn Binance hack

US crime agencies, including the Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI) and Secret Service, have been assisting Binance in tracing the crypto taken from the platform's own wallets.

The agencies, along with a range of cyber analysts, said that the hack - like similar breaches before it - bore the signs of having been orchestrated by North Korea.

Pyongyang hit back at the claims on Tuesday, denying it was behind the hack.

Kim Yo Jong, a top Democratic People's Republic of Korea official and the sister of leader Kim Jong Un, said on the accusations were another "sinister attempt" by the US to damage North Korea's public image.

The country has consistently denied being behind a number of major cyber attacks it has been blamed for in recent years.

The TraderTraitor unit, which is also known as the Lazarus Group, was blamed for a then-record $1.5bn hack of crypto exchange Bybit in 2025.

Binance had previously not responded to the BBC's queries about North Korea's alleged involvement in the hack.

But on Tuesday, the company wrote in its post on X, external: "The latest information released by the Federal Bureau of Investigation indicates that the Democratic People's Republic of Korea is responsible for this incident."

As part of its security efforts following the attack, Binance said it would also increase investment in AI tools that can detect security threats, as well as engage third-party cyber experts to carry out a full-scale review of the incident.

It also said that it would seek to stablish a security research fund and work with leading AI and security firms on targeted initiatives.

#132. CrowdStrike's new kill switch can stop rogue AI agents in their tracks, CEO says

CrowdStrike CEO George Kurtz said Tuesday that the cybersecurity company can now shut down rogue artificial intelligence agents.

CrowdStrike's new kill switch, announced Tuesday at its Fal.Con Europe conference, allows security teams to terminate misbehaving agents, prevent them from restarting and cut off their network access. It is designed to give humans more power over artificial intelligence models and systems, as concerns about how to control agents grow.

"Everyone is building AI agents, but they never had the off button, and now they do," Kurtz told CNBC's Jim Cramer on "Mad Money." "It's going to be an incredibly important part of making sure that AI agents run and operate safely."

The AI Kill Switch, as CrowdStrike calls it, is part of the company's Falcon Guardian platform, which monitors AI agents operating within an organization. The technology, which is available immediately, is aimed at addressing AI safety concerns surrounding the rapid adoption of AI agents.

In September, Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei called for slowing AI development over safety concerns, a position backed by OpenAI CEO Sam Altman. Kurtz argued at the time that slowing AI development would not eliminate existing security risks, which he said underscored the need for safeguards as companies deploy autonomous systems.

Kurtz said Tuesday that CrowdStrike's new kill switch represents a significant shift in cybersecurity, which has traditionally focused on protecting companies from external threats. As businesses rely more on AI agents, he argued that security providers must also ensure those systems operate safely within their own organizations.

"Enabling agents to work safely is a massive market opportunity for CrowdStrike," Kurtz said. "We have AI capabilities to prevent these breaches from happening on the outside, and we also have the capabilities to make sure that agents are basically doing what they are instructed to do."

The CEO compared the technology to airbags in cars, arguing that safeguards are necessary even when AI systems are designed to operate correctly.

"If they go outside of those guardrails at runtime, we can kill those agents," he said.

CrowdStrike is seeing growing demand for its security tools as businesses adopt AI. Kurtz pointed to CrowdStrike's partnership with Anthropic to protect its Claude Cowork product: He said enterprise customers had pushed for CrowdStrike's technology to be integrated before they deployed Anthropic's offerings.

"Our customers went to Anthropic and said, 'We need CrowdStrike before we can deploy AI,'" Kurtz said. "I think it says volumes that customers would not roll out AI without CrowdStrike."

Shares of CrowdStrike closed at a record high of $278.86 on Oct. 6 but have since fallen about 12% as tech stocks struggled in October and into November.

#130. Baked by Melissa enters Target with first at-home cupcake mix

Baked by Melissa will enter the at-home baking aisle in Target stores as the retailer tries to persuade shoppers to spend more at its locations this holiday season.

As of Sunday, Target is exclusively selling the brand's cupcake kits, liners and a cupcake pan, all in the dessert company's signature bite-size portions. The line includes three cupcake flavors: Christmas Tie-Dye, Marshmallow Hot Cocoa and Red Velvet.

The company's entry into at-home baking comes as a number of restaurants turn to consumer packaged goods to boost sales in an otherwise challenging time for the industry.

Some players, such as California Pizza Kitchen and TGI Fridays, have licensed their brands for frozen foods for decades. But other restaurant chains, including Chick-fil-A and Dutch Bros, have leaned into grocery products much more recently. For smaller companies, such as Momofuku or Carbone, space on a grocery shelf helps extend brand reach and gives consumers a chance to try their food without a reservation and at a much lower cost.

Founded by Melissa Ben-Ishay in 2008, Baked by Melissa has nine permanent locations in the New York area and ships its minicupcakes nationwide.

The regional chain has garnered attention outside the city thanks to Ben-Ishay's social media presence. On TikTok, the Baked by Melissa account has more than 2.8 million followers, fueled by popular recipe videos, such as for her green goddess salad dressing. Ben-Ishay's social media stardom has led to collaborations with Ferrero and Wonder.

Baked by Melissa had discussed at-home baking products for years, but Ben-Ishay, who is also president of the company, only decided to take the plunge a year ago during a trip to Target with her two kids, she told CNBC. A spin through the baking aisle made her decide Baked by Melissa "should be here," she said.

"I love baking cake mix. I truly do. And, quite frankly, sometimes I just think it tastes better," Ben-Ishay said.

But retail moves more slowly than Baked by Melissa's usual pace.

The dessert company needed just a few days to create New York Knicks-branded minicupcakes after the team won the NBA championship in June, for example, but figuring out retail products meant "a lot of waiting and approvals and new partners," Ben-Ishay said.

Twelve months later, the cupcake mixes have arrived on store shelves, just in time for the all-important holiday season.

For Target, the next two months will be critical as the retailer tries to woo back its core customer after a disappointing holiday season last year. That prototypical Target shopper often looks a lot like Ben-Ishay.

"We started with Target because I'm a 42-year-old mom of two, and I find myself there a lot," she said.

#129. No more rice grains in Kellogg's Rice Krispies due to changing weather

Breakfast cereal giant Kellogg's has stopped using rice grains in Rice Krispies and Coco Pops in the UK due to "changing weather conditions" that have disrupted supplies.

Corn flour and rice flour are being used instead, a Kellogg's spokesperson said on Tuesday, adding that the updated recipes have been "carefully assessed" and the firm has taken customer feedback into consideration.

Those ingredients are replacing rice grains as crops have been hit by dry weather in key producing countries in Asia, reducing harvests and driving up prices.

The new recipes mean cereal packaging will now include a "contains wheat gluten" warning.

Rice Krispies products in the US and Canada will continue to be made with real rice with no changes to their recipes expected, a spokesperson for Kellogg's North American business told the BBC.

The recipe change in the UK will help "maintain consistent quality at a time when changing weather conditions are affecting the quality and availability of some of the rice and grains used in our cereals," a Kellogg's spokesperson told the BBC.

"We appreciate that even small changes to a much-loved product can feel noticeable and that's why we have approached this with a lot of care and consideration."

The company also said that Rice Krispies and Coco Pops will continue to meet UK food standards on levels of fat, salt and sugar in food.

The cereals also contain no artificial colours, flavours or sweeteners and the nutritional benefits in each bowl, such as fibre, Vitamin D and iron are staying the same, the spokesperson added.

Watch: How does El NinÌo affect world weather?

The Kellogg company separated into two distinct firms in 2023.

The largest is Kellanova, which focuses on snacks, cereals, noodles and other food products like Eggo, Pringles and Pop-Tarts sold globally. It is now owned by Mars, which makes confectionery and pet food.

The other business, WK Kellogg's, operates in North America and has since been taken over by Italian chocolate giant Ferrero.

Most of the world's rice comes from Asia, many parts of which has been impacted by droughts, floods and heatwaves due to the El NiÃ±o weather phenomenon.

The weather event is expected to severely impact crop production from South East Asia in particular. The region produces more than a quarter of the world's rice.

The World Meteorological Agency expects the record-breaking El NiÃ±o will last until at least 2027.

#128. Binance breach puts custody at center of crypto infrastructure trade

The $6.3 billion Binance hack could accelerate a shift in how investors value cryptocurrency companies, putting a greater premium on custody providers and the infrastructure needed to securely hold and protect digital assets.

Much of crypto's evolution this year has involved companies moving past their reliance on trading volumes and transaction fees, to focus instead on building the infrastructure connecting digital assets with traditional finance. Last Thursday's breach adds a new dimension, bringing cybersecurity, custody, compliance and balance-sheet resilience into sharper focus.

Importantly, the Binance breach was not an indictment of crypto itself, or a case of leadership-driven fraud, as with FTX in late 2022. Instead, Binance's leadership moved quickly to contain the fallout, protect customers and commit the exchange's own resources to cover losses.

On Tuesday, Binance CEO Richard Teng told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" that the company has replenished 98% of $6.34 billion in affected assets, and that it is still in the process of purchasing outstanding, more illiquid assets.

Management's response, alongside efforts elsewhere in the industry to boost confidence, helped prevent a security breach from escalating into a broader crisis of confidence. For example, Tether, Circle, Ripple and others moved quickly to freeze or restrict access to about $3 billion in stolen assets; Coinbase blocked transfers to compromised wallets and offered Binance direct assistance.

The hack could even help shape the next phase of the crypto infrastructure trade. The new winners may not just be the businesses with the most users, volume or assets under custody, but the ones that can also demonstrate they're able to protect those assets under extreme duress.

"Which company has the resources to invest in cybersecurity and protect customer funds? For a smaller company, it could be much harder because once these crypto companies become infrastructure plays, providing the well for tokenization … they need to have the compliance and control in place so that a similar hacking will not happen or can be minimized," Owen Lau, senior analyst at ClearStreet, told CNBC. "Larger companies have more resources that could give them some advantage."

While institutional investors have increasingly embraced digital assets, broader adoption depends on confidence that those assets can be securely held, accessed and transferred. The Binance breach highlights the importance of that infrastructure, and could increase demand for specialized custodians and platforms with established asset-protection capabilities.

Coinbase has operated a dedicated institutional custody business since 2018. Circle entered the custody business this year, after the U.S. Office of the Comptroller of the Currency granted it approval to operate as a trust bank. In the past few years, Ripple and Kraken have also launched custody businesses.

Still, smaller players compete in the space as well. Small cap crypto exchange stock Gemini Space Station has offered custody services since 2019. BitGo Holdings, founded in 2013, is one of the earliest players in institutional crypto custody and infrastructure, with a reputation for security and serving major institutions.

The shift could also drive consolidation as companies look to buy capabilities, deepen their infrastructure offerings and achieve the scale needed to compete, Lau said.

The more consequential structural shift may be the separation of custody from execution, according to Morgan Stanley.

"Crypto exchanges have historically combined trading, custody, and settlement within vertically integrated platforms, concentrating operational risk. Repeated security breaches could encourage investors to move assets toward independent custody while continuing to trade on CEXs," the firm's analyst Felix Stratmann said in a note Monday," referring to centralized cryptocurrency exchanges.

"This would preserve the role of centralized exchanges as liquidity and execution venues, while potentially benefiting regulated custodians, including banks, as institutional investors place greater emphasis on asset segregation, operational resilience, and investor protection," Stratmann said.

#126. UK and EU agree draft deal to scrap Brexit red tape over food exports

The UK and EU have agreed a draft deal on removing post-Brexit checks from food and agriculture exports, ahead of the UK-EU summit later this month.

The BBC understands the 200-page draft text for this so called "SPS" [sanitary and phytosanitary] agreement has been circulated to EU nations in Brussels for consideration at a meeting on Wednesday.

Similar texts for deals on emissions trading and a youth travel and work scheme have also been circulated alongside a 15-page draft joint statement for UK and EU top leaders.

The food deal is the most significant part of the so-called Brexit reset agreed so far, removing checks and red tape that hit exporters after the UK exited the EU's single market.

As a result of the agreement, the government claims the UK economy will eventually see Â£5.1bn worth of annual benefits.

Speaking during the Commonwealth Heads of Government Meeting (Chogm) in Antigua and Barbuda, the prime minister said a UK-EU deal could add up to 0.4% extra on GDP.

The deal could have the following impact:

Exports to the EU of fresh sausages, burgers, shellfish from domestic waters and seed potatoes would be able to resume

It should remove the need for Â£200 Export Health Certificates, which confirm the product meets the health requirements of the country it is heading to

It should also end the need for Â£25 Phytosanitary certificates, which show that plants or plant products are free from pests and diseases

Inspector fees, organic certificates, some port fees and other checks for exports and imports to and from the EU could be removed

It will bring back passports for British pets being taken into the EU

In return, the UK is expected to commit to observing and adapting to existing EU regulations on plant, animal and food trade standards - a process called "dynamic alignment".

Each of the elements on the table was up for discussion under Sir Keir Starmer, and the summit would have taken place by now had Labour not replaced its leader in July.

Under Andy Burnham, Britain's approach to post-Brexit relations with the EU have shifted significantly. Sir Keir said the UK would not rejoin the bloc in his lifetime, while Burnham has said he would like to see Britain back in the EU in his.

At Chogm on Tuesday, Burnham said: "I think people in Britain would expect me to take that with both hands, given where we've been in recent times, and that's what I'm doing."

Pressed on whether it would be acceptable for the UK to have its rules on food trade made by the EU, he said: "We're not in the EU, therefore we do have to work with them in a different way.

"But if that can get growth, if that can help our farmers, if that can remove red tape from British businesses, I think most people would expect me to go to that summit and negotiate the best deal that I can.

"And that's what I've been doing in recent weeks, leaning in, being positive, having a really constructive relationship with our European partners and hopefully we can land a good deal for Britain in terms of growth but also I hope more collaboration on controlling immigration."

Jon Walsh of Bio&Me said his firm had stopped exporting yoghurt due to vets' fees

Jon Walsh, the chief executive and co-founder of Bio&Me, which makes granolas, mueslis and yoghurts, told BBC 5 Live the draft deal sounded like "really good news".

"We're just a small company but when we were trying to export our yoghurts before, every time we'd send a pallet we had to pay a vet to inspect it and that was Â£140, and that meant that you'd lose money... so we had to stop."

The company also had problems exporting granola and overnight oats, with some shipments being returned.

"It was so frustrating because it was the same person filling out the same form and yet half would make it through and half wouldn't," Walsh said.

"You just kind of give up because you're losing money."

In an interview with ITV, Burnham said "public opinion has changed nationally" since the 2016 Brexit vote, but added he would "not run ahead of public opinion" on the issue.

"The public need to be the boss when it comes to whatever happens with Brexit," he added.

Burnham also said he had not proposed the return of freedom of movement between the UK and EU.

Asked by the BBC about the UK's relationship with the EU, Tory Leader Kemi Badenoch said that while we should "work closely", "dynamic alignment" with the EU would be a red line and "actually take us backwards".

Liberal Democrat Europe spokesperson Al Pinkerton said the draft deal was a "patch, not a fix".

"The real solution is to rejoin the single market and to establish a customs union with the EU," he added.

Reform UK's business spokesman Richard Tice said: "One of the key benefits of Brexit was that Britain no longer had to adhere to ridiculous EU rules.

"Returning to any form of alignment with Europe would crush Britain's competitiveness and force us to surrender sovereignty over trade policy, putting trade deals worth hundreds of billions of pounds at risk."

European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen with Prime Minister Andy Burnham

While UK rules remain mostly aligned with EU ones, there are some areas where England has diverged from the EU rule book, for example on "precision breeding" of plants, previously known as gene editing. In recent weeks, agreement has been struck on transition arrangements while the EU's new regulations come in to force.

The government has already announced a European Partnerships Bill which will provide a legal mechanism for the application of new EU rules and regulations. This will also allow for the necessary alignments for the linking of the UK emissions trading scheme with the EU's, and save the need for UK exporters to pay the EU's new carbon border tax.

The draft agreements will be discussed at a meeting of EU ambassadors called Coreper on Wednesday, with some key details on the planned numbers in a Youth Experience Scheme to be finalised at a political level in the coming days. A discount on full foreign student fees for EU students is likely to be pushed to next year alongside a plan to reconnect electricity markets.

The BBC understands the draft agreements will also include some references to UK involvement in defence co-operation schemes, access to law enforcement databases and some reassurances over the EU's "Made in Europe" plans.

A food deal will have particular significance for Northern Ireland, radically reducing the impact of the post-Brexit Irish Sea border.

Northern Ireland's Brexit deal kept it inside the EU's single market for goods. That means food products from elsewhere in the UK require paperwork and some physical checks when arriving at NI's ports. A deal will ultimately end those requirements.

While the food trade plans have been backed by supermarkets and farmers representatives, some pro-Brexit campaigners have criticised the reapplication of EU rules. The post-Brexit trade deal signed by Boris Johnson's government prioritised regulatory freedoms for the UK over avoiding new regulatory red tape, checks and delays.

But William Bain, the British Chamber of Commerce's (BCC) head of trade policy, told the BBC's Today programme that business overall thought it was a "price is worth paying" to follow common rules, as companies would benefit from "lower costs, reduced delays [and] increased market access".

At the moment, he said, the requirement for Export Health Certificates can add thousands of pounds of costs to each truck carrying goods, in addition "to the delays that people find at the ports or at the borders".

"So we get lower exports because of the delays and the massive increases in costs that have come into the supply chain."

The BCC has released a new analysis of its members' views suggesting options for a closer relationship.

The business lobby group says the current trade relationship is "failing to deliver", leaving firms "struggling with a thicket of Brexit red tape" nearly six years after the UK's exit from the bloc.

#125. Ferrari raises 2026 guidance after Q3 earnings beat

Ferrari on Tuesday raised its 2026 guidance after the sports car maker slightly beat Wall Street's third-quarter expectations.

Here's how Ferrari performed in the third quarter compared with average estimates compiled by LSEG:

Ferrari raised its guidance to revenue of 7.65 billion euros, up from 7.6 billion euros; adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization to 3 billion euros, up from 2.97 billion euros; and adjusted EPS to at least 9.85 euros, up from at least 9.68 euros.

The automaker also upgraded its expectations for industrial free cash flow to up to 1.65 billion euros, up from previous expectations for at least 1.55 billion euros.

This is the second consecutive quarter Ferrari has raised its 2026 guidance, despite vehicle shipments being lower by 318 units through the first nine months of the year, at 10,170 vehicles, compared with last year. The decline is led by fewer shipments to the Americas and China, Hong Kong and Taiwan.

Ferrari said Tuesday that the guidance improvements were a result of stronger-than-expected customer personalizations, lower than anticipated currency headwinds and a second place finish in the Formula 1 championship.

"Our order book now reaches into 2028, and we still have two more launches to come this year. In addition, we continue to create engaging events around the world to further tighten the bonds with our clients and partners across Racing, Sports Cars and Lifestyle," Ferrari CEO Benedetto Vigna said in a release.

RBC Capital Markets analyst Tom Narayan said Tuesday that Ferrari's results were largely in line with Wall Street expectations. However, he said shares may be "under slight pressure given some were hoping for a bigger guidance raise, but expect it to get better once folks realize these results are healthy."

Ferrari's U.S. stock initially declined on the results before turning positive to close Tuesday at $414.77 a share, up 2.9%.
