#123. Binance co-CEO Richard Teng says hackers used fake job interviews, AI tool to steal $6.3 billion in crypto

Binance said hackers used fake job interviews, malicious software and compromised internal AI tools to infiltrate its wallet infrastructure, leading to the record-breaking Oct. 29 cyberattack that resulted in more than $6 billion in assets being stolen from the world's largest crypto exchange.

The attackers "spent months building their way in," Binance co-CEO Richard Teng told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Tuesday, rejecting suggestions that there was a single point of failure through one employee's laptop.

"In June, [the attackers] set up a fake version of a developer tool and lured one of our engineers to install that," he added. "In September they posed as recruiters and ran a series of job interviews with another engineer, and that ended up in a coding exercise that contained hidden malware designed to evade security ... from there they moved violently through our internal AI tools and obtain[ed] access that breach[ed] the system that authorized our wallet transaction."

He also said Binance has replenished 98% of $6.34 billion in affected assets, and that it is still in the process of purchasing outstanding, more illiquid assets.

Teng himself did not attribute the attack to North Korea but acknowledged that the FBI has linked it to North Korean hackers tied to the TraderTraitor group.

Preparations for the attack began in June, when a Binance Wallet DevOps engineer downloaded a tampered Homebrew installation "to install a popular AI coding tool after searching for it on a search engine," according to a security report published by Binance Tuesday. It said it detected the suspicious activity, replaced the engineer's device and rotated credentials as a precaution, but that by then the employee's "role and his related colleagues on our wallet infrastructure team was exposed to the hacker."

As Binance works to recover from the incident, it has temporarily restricted certain withdrawals, pledged to cover all customer losses and tapped its own reserves to replenish affected assets. Binance and industry partners including Tether, Circle and Ripple have reportedly frozen about $3 billion in assets, though some stolen funds continue to move through laundered channels. Coinbase blocked transfers to compromised wallets and offered Binance direct assistance.

Although the incident represents a significant security breach, Binance maintains that it remains financially sound and focused on identifying the vulnerability, recovering remaining funds and preventing another attack.

The hack sparked a sharp but short-lived hit to confidence across crypto markets, especially compared with previous failures at major exchanges. Investors are now watching whether Binance can fully restore confidence in centralized exchanges.

As of Tuesday Binance has rebuilt the affected wallet system and internal AI tool, replaced compromised devices, decommissioned credentials and tokens associated with the affected engineers and expanded security scans across employee devices, Teng told "Squawk on the Street."

"We're also getting an independent expert to review everything that we have done, and we'll keep strengthening our defenses as the investigation continues," Teng said. "We're working around the clock with law enforcement agencies and with industry partners to trace and freeze the stolen funds for that front."

#122. Poundland supplier 'will lose millions' if retailer collapses

Adam Turner is sales director at Poundland supplier Hillcrest

One of Poundland's suppliers has said it will lose millions if the firm collapses.

While that supplier will stay afloat, it is feared that some small and medium-sized suppliers could go under.

This is in addition to potentially thousands of job losses among staff.

Politicians have been putting questions to representatives of Poundland owners Gordon Brothers this week seeking "clarity" on plans for the retailer.

Adam Turner, sales director at Highcrest Retail Brands - a family owned Bradford business - told the BBC that Poundland was one of Highcrest's biggest customers, and that the firms have had a relationship for decades.

Highcrest mainly sells confectionary to Poundland, and turns over about Â£50m.

It will "lose a few million" if Poundland becomes insolvent, so it will have a gap in its balance sheet next year, he said.

Highcrest was a "longstanding business that can stomach this", but other medium and small suppliers have told him that "if Poundland goes under, so do they", Mr Turner said.

This echoes a warning from Poundland founder Steve Smith on Monday that two or three thousand suppliers to the firm, which only work for Poundland, would be directly affected by its collapse.

Not only 12,000 Poundland staff would be affected, but also potentially tens of thousands more, he said.

If Poundland's stores are sold to new buyers, some of the employees may get new jobs on the same premises. However, it is understood that buyers are only interested in about half the stores at present - meaning roughly 6,000 employees would not be re-employed.

Poundland's founder offers to invest in firm

Mr Turner said that the last three months of the year is the "golden quarter" for retailers. In terms of sweets, Poundland will get its Halloween stock in from July, and Christmas stock in from September.

These three months will be the time of year when suppliers will be owed the most by Poundland.

If Poundland goes under, suppliers will be getting "pennies in the pound" back from the administration, he said, which is "very upsetting" - although he still hopes a deal can be done so Poundland can remain solvent.

Poundland owners Gordon Brothers will be a secured creditor, which means they are first in line to get their money back, and suppliers "who have done nothing wrong" will take the financial hit.

"It's sad. It's a nightmare for us all to be honest with you. It's not just the suppliers, it's the staff at Poundland, some of these guys have been there for many years.

"It's from the warehouse staff all the way to the shop staff to everybody within head office. It's a massive ripple effect through the supply chain," he added.

Mr Turner said he had no knowledge of whether Gordon Brothers had deliberately timed the process of Poundland going into administration so it could take advantage of having high stock levels, which it can then sell.

One of the ways Gordon Brothers makes money is through stock liquidations - selling off stock from collapsed businesses.

One insider familiar with the situation told the BBC that the timing of Poundland facing collapse was "odd" - most retailers don't fall over in the run-up to Christmas.

Gordon Brothers was approached for comment.

Meanwhile, business secretary Jonathan Reynolds will meet with Gordon Brothers later this week after he and Prime Minister Andy Burnham questioned why a solvent deal had not been made.

Business minster Kate Dearden told MPs that government officials had also met with representatives from Gordon Brothers on Monday.

"I am deeply concerned about the uncertainty surrounding Poundland's future and the impact it could have on its staff and their families who are at the forefront of my mind," Dearden told the House of Commons.

"Poundland is not currently in administration and the business continues to trade, but this is a worrying time for employees, suppliers and communities", she added.

The minister confirmed she would also be meeting with Poundland executives on Wednesday.

She said the government doesn't want to interfere with "current ongoing commercial negotiations", but said ministers would be seeking "clarity" on the plans for the retailer.

"It's absolutely legitimate for this government to ask the business if they're doing everything they can to protect employees and suppliers up and down the country," she added.

Dearden told MPs that the government would be ready to help the company's employees, should the current situation end in administration.

"We stand ready to support them if they need to move into new employment, however... our priority is always in protecting jobs and the Secretary of State and I will continue to engage with the company to secure the best possible outcome," she added.

The head of Gordon Brothers' European operations reportedly told the government that the firm stood by its stewardship and had given Poundland Â£85m in additional financing since its purchase in 2025.

#121. AMD stock jumps 8% on earnings as data center revenue nearly doubles

Advanced Micro Devices reported third-quarter earnings on Tuesday that beat Wall Street's expectations, with revenue jumping 45% and data center sales nearly doubling from a year earlier. The stock was up 8% in extended trading.

Here's how the chipmaker did versus LSEG consensus estimates for the quarter:

Revenue increased from $9.25 billion a year earlier, AMD said in a statement. Neoclouds like CoreWeave and Nebius rallied earlier Tuesday and remained higher after AMD reported results.

CEO Lisa Su said on the earnings call that she expects server revenue to double year over year in 2027 and data center AI growth to accelerate even faster than the company had projected 90 days ago. The company expects data center to drive the majority of its revenue and earnings growth.

"We have worked across our supply chain to secure the wafers, substrates, advanced packaging, memory, and other key components needed to support much higher growth next year," Su said on the earnings call.

AMD's report comes days after Intel disclosed similar data center growth, with revenue in that segment up 78% in the third quarter. But Intel's stock plummeted 29% on Friday over concerns about weak guidance and Intel's ability to meet demand.

The data center unit makes up the majority of AMD's business, accounting for 64% of overall revenue, up from 47% a year ago.

After years spent chasing Nvidia in the market for artificial intelligence graphics processing units, or GPUs, AMD's central processing units, or CPUs, are experiencing a major resurgence with the rise of agentic AI apps like Meta's Muse. AMD's server CPUs are key for agentic apps because they power virtual machines and can run for hours on their own. Demand from agentic AI boosted AMD shares to a record in October.

"Adoption is just starting, and we expect demand to grow sharply as agents are deployed more broadly and take on more complex work," Su said on the earnings call with analysts.

AMD's stock is up about 125% this year, but it saw three consecutive days of losses before rallying on Tuesday amid an industrywide sell-off tied to fears about overspending on AI.

AMD is also a major player in the weakening PC market, which Intel blamed for its poor outlook last week. AMD and Intel are the largest makers of traditional x86 processors that power many laptops and desktops, while other laptops rely on Arm-based processors from the likes of Qualcomm and Apple.

Global PC shipments declined 4% in the second quarter from a year earlier due to soaring costs for memory and other components, according to Counterpoint Research.

AMD reports PC chip sales in a segment combined with gaming chips. Revenue in that business fell 8% from a year earlier to $3.7 billion. Gaming revenue was down 40% from a year ago.

For the fourth quarter, AMD said it expects about $17 billion in revenue, beating the $16.2 billion average analyst estimate, according to LSEG. AMD sees its gross margin for the quarter coming in at 55%, slightly below the average estimate of 55.3%, according to StreetAccount.

AMD also sells data center GPUs and full rack-scale systems called Helios that compete directly with Nvidia's Grace Blackwell and Vera Rubin systems. Meta, Microsoft, Anthropic, OpenAI, and Oracle are all beginning to use AMD Helios in their data centers. Meta said last week that its Helios racks are up and running, and AMD agreed to invest $1 billion in Meta's equity offering.

AMD has also committed to investing up to $5 billion in Anthropic, which unveiled its highly anticipated prospectus last week as it inches closer to a potentially historic initial public offering.

During a visit to Taiwan in October, Su said she plans to "substantially increase our supply in 2027." Su and Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang have both made multiple trips to Taiwan and South Korea this year, holding meetings with key suppliers like Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company and memory leaders SK Hynix and Samsung.

In September, AMD said it would acquire World Labs for $8.2 billion in an all-stock deal. The San Francisco-based AI lab, founded by industry pioneer Fei-Fei Li, is developing a so-called world model that simulates 3D environments to aid in developing robots and other physically grounded AI applications.

#119. Versant earnings beat expectations as digital platforms drive growth

Versant Media on Tuesday reported quarterly earnings and revenue that beat expectations and reaffirmed its full-year 2026 guidance, citing strength across its portfolio and growth in its platforms revenue.

The media company reported $245 million in revenue from digital platforms including Fandango, marking a nearly 10% year-over-year increase. Versant said Fandango saw "significant growth" in ticketing revenue in the third quarter, notching its highest level since the pandemic.

Advertising revenue, however, slipped to $373 million during the period, down 5.3% from last year, primarily due to the impact of last year's Ryder Cup, according to the company.

The Versant portfolio includes CNBC, MS NOW, Golf Channel and other cable and digital properties.

Here's how the company performed in its third quarter, compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Versant completed four major distribution renewals this year following its spinoff from Comcast's NBCUniversal.

During the third quarter, Versant said, its networks reached more than 120 million viewers monthly and its aggregate viewership grew by double digits year over year. The company also launched MS NOW's direct-to-consumer product and Fandango's on-demand product as new growth drivers. CNBC is expected to launch a direct-to-consumer product next year, Versant said.

For the period ended Sept. 30, Versant recorded net income of $47 million, or 34 cents per share, compared with $80 million, or 56 cents per share, the year prior.

Adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization was $353 million, beating Wall Street estimates of $305 million, according to StreetAccount.

Revenue fell slightly to $1.59 billion from $1.66 billion in the year-ago period. The company said its revenue figure included the acquisition of golf simulation company Full Swing and the sale of sports management software company SportsEngine.

The company reaffirmed its full-year revenue outlook of between $6.2 billion and $6.45 billion and its adjusted EBITDA outlook of $1.9 billion to $2.05 billion. It adjusted part of its full-year guidance, saying it now expects free cash flow to exceed the prior range of between $1 billion and $1.2 billion.

"We're building on strong relationships we already have with consumers, advertisers and distributors," Lazarus said on the call. "We're finding new ways to reach and serve those audiences. We have the brands, the financial strength and the people to execute this transition successfully."

Versant began trading as a standalone company on the Nasdaq in January after separating from Comcast. Though it began largely as a portfolio of cable networks, Versant executives have said they intend to diversify the company's revenue mix to eventually draw 50% from digital, platform, subscription, ad-supported and transactional businesses.

Live sports continues to be a major driver for the company. Versant said it saw "robust audience engagement" in the third quarter and that WNBA viewership on Versant's USA Sports networks was 25% higher than the prior year's cable average.

Versant also announced an additional $100 million accelerated stock repurchase program beginning Wednesday. It expects that transaction to be completed during the fourth quarter.

Disclosure: Versant Media is the parent company of CNBC.

#117. Apple warns of risks related to AI agents behaving in 'unintended or unpredictable' ways

Apple said in its annual financial filing on Tuesday that AI agents that can act in "unintended or unpredictable ways" could represent a threat to its business by opening the company up to lawsuits or hurting its reputation.

The new risk factors in Apple's filing show the disruptive potential of AI agents, which have rapidly become top of mind for Silicon Valley's most important companies.

"In particular, artificial intelligence agents and other technologies can take actions autonomously, including interacting with internal or external systems that in some cases they may not be authorized to access, executing or altering code, sending communications, and completing transactions," Apple wrote in the filing.

The company added that agents "may behave in unintended or unpredictable ways, including by taking actions that were not directed, instructed or authorized."

Earlier this year, OpenAI released Dots, and Meta introduced Muse. Both AI agents use advanced AI models to work autonomously for tasks. They can even email or remind users when new messages arrive.

Apple's disclosure comes after several episodes that involved rogue AI agents breaking out of their sandboxes to hack third-party websites, like the Hugging Face incident, which has drawn renewed attention from policymakers in Washington, D.C., and around the world.

Separately, the ability of agents to use a computer in human ways is forcing big companies like Amazon and JPMorgan Chase to come up with policies around agentic access to services for strategic or security reasons.

Last month, Apple announced changes to full disk access on Macs to reduce "risks related to this level of access" for AI agents installed on PCs.

"The Company's ability to oversee and control third-party artificial intelligence agents and other technologies may be limited," Apple warned in its filing.

Apple doesn't currently have an agentic AI. But when new CEO John Ternus was asked on Monday's earnings call about AI agents, he said that the company had "real room" to expand Siri's capabilities.

This fall, Apple released Siri AI, a new assistant that uses the iPhone's local data storage to access data like calendars and emails, but Apple's Siri can't work autonomously for long stretches like Dots or Muse.

Ternus said Apple can't access private data on its devices, calling it a "core differentiator."

"If you think about it, so much of your personal data is on your iPhone, whether it's your contacts, your communications, health data, financial data, all of that," Ternus said. "Siri can tap into that and help you access it in a way that's very secure and private and that nobody else has access to."

Commercial AI agents like those from OpenAI and Meta ask for high-level access to the user's accounts like email in order to be more useful.

Apple indicated that the current regulations around AI and "super intelligence" are changing, unpredictable and that it could cost a lot of money to adapt to new laws governing AI.

"In addition, the Company is subject to the risks posed by ethical concerns and negative consumer perceptions related to automation, artificial intelligence and super intelligence, including perceived threats to safety or other societal harms or complications," according to the filing.

#116. Yum Brands earnings beat estimates, but cyclospora outbreak weighs on Taco Bell

Yum Brands on Tuesday reported earnings that beat expectations, but a cyclospora outbreak linked to iceberg lettuce at Taco Bell weighed on the company's same-store sales growth.

However, Yum said Taco Bell, which the company considers a "growth engine" for its business, has since rebounded.

"Since the end of September, Taco Bell U.S. has been outpacing the [quick-service restaurant] industry on same-store sales growth," Yum CEO Chris Turner said in a statement.

Taco Bell's same-store sales are up about 1% so far in the fourth quarter, CFO Ranjith Roy said on the company's earnings conference call Tuesday. In the last four weeks ended Friday, same-store sales have risen 3% compared with the year-ago period.

Here's what the company reported compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Yum reported fiscal third-quarter net income of $1.62 billion, or $5.92 per share, up from $397 million, or $1.41 per share, a year earlier.

Excluding gains from its divestiture of Pizza Hut and other items, the restaurant company earned $1.56 per share.

During the quarter, Yum completed its divestiture of Pizza Hut. It closed the sale of the pizza chain's business in mainland China to Yum China on Aug. 7 and sold the rest of Pizza Hut to LongRange Capital on Sept. 1. The company's third-quarter results include Pizza Hut up until those dates.

Excluding Pizza Hut, Yum's same-store sales were flat for the quarter as Taco Bell weighed on the company's overall results. Taco Bell's same-store sales declined by 4% as diners temporarily pulled back on visits due to the foodborne illness outbreak.

Yum executives previously said the weekend of July 18 saw the most severe impact to sales, but trends have improved since then.

Taco Bell's most loyal customers returned faster, Yum said, thanks in part to weekly Tuesday deals for loyalty program members after the cyclospora outbreak took over headlines.

Traffic from "medium" and "light" customers has bounced back to about 95% of prior levels, while visits from "heavy" users is back to normal, Roy said. To attract more casual diners, Taco Bell has been relying on star power, like a collaboration with pop star Olivia Rodrigo on a pink-colored Mountain Dew Baja Blast.

Outside of the U.S., Taco Bell's much smaller international business reported same-store sales growth of 5%.

KFC's same-store sales ticked up 1%, helped by system sales growth in China and Europe. The U.S., now the chicken chain's third-largest market, saw system sales shrink 6% in the quarter.

Like Restaurant Brands International's Popeyes, KFC has struggled in the U.S. amid a wave of new competitors and more budget-conscious consumers. Its home market represents only about 5% of KFC's operating profit, but it remains a symbolically important part of the business for Yum investors.

During the quarter, KFC U.S. launched popcorn chicken, which sold out in many restaurants. However, Yum said the limited-time menu item was not enough to offset the successful Hot & Spicy Wings and Potato Wedges promotion during the year-ago period.

Globally, KFC is trying to become "the most modern chicken QSR," Turner said on the company's earnings conference call. To achieve that lofty goal, he said the chain will roll out buzzy promotions, a new brand identity, and a wider menu that includes upgraded sauces and more beverages.

#114. What the 2026 midterm elections could mean for Trump's final two years

Tuesday's midterm elections will at their heart determine control of Congress for the remainder of Donald Trump's presidency. U.S. voters will decide whether Trump will maintain his Republican iron grip on Washington or recede into lame-duck status after a Democratic triumph.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm elections

If Republicans win, Trump could spend another two years facing little congressional resistance and pushing through major policy packages. If Democrats win one or both chambers, the president's remaining legislative priorities would be severely kneecapped, and even the basic functions of government could become gridlocked.

Oddsmakers and public polls placed Democrats in a favorable position ahead of Election Day, especially for flipping control of the Republican-held House. Still, the balance of power will not be decided until ballots are counted — a process that could take days or, in some cases, weeks to complete. The Senate is expected to be particularly close, and if the balance of power comes down to Alaska, the nation may have to wait while the state counts.

"The election results could trigger an immediate market reaction, particularly as the Senate outcome becomes clearer," the Washington Policy Strategy Group of Stifel wrote Sunday in an analyst report, where it predicted Democrats will win the House while the Senate remains too close to call. "However, the market's knee-jerk trade could reverse as investors recognize that gridlock remains the most likely policy outcome in either case."

Republicans also control the Senate and with a larger majority than in the House.

If Democrats capture one chamber of Congress, Washington policymaking would become significantly more gridlocked. Single-party budget reconciliation bills, the procedural vehicle for most major spending and policy changes in the current status quo of tight congressional margins, would become impossible. Bipartisanship would be required to advance legislation, making must-pass bills such as government funding packages, defense spending and the looming debt ceiling hike more important — and more contentious.

In a divided Congress, the party that doesn't control the White House typically uses moments including government funding and debt-ceiling hikes to extract concessions from the majority party, a practice that can lead to brinksmanship.

"If Republicans defy the conventional wisdom and maintain their Senate majority, then we would expect a relief rally in the sectors that might react negatively to a Democratic victory," Stifel wrote. "Democrats would still govern the House and gridlock would prevail. In this scenario, Senate Republicans would act as a check on both the Democrats and many of Trump's populist impulses."

Democrats are also widely expected to use any majority in Congress to launch investigations into the White House and businesses that have worked with the Trump administration. The majority party gains subpoena power in congressional committees.

Polling indicates a Democratic sweep of the House and Senate is possible. In that case, Democrats may more aggressively pursue party-line reconciliation measures that wouldn't become law but would give the party a megaphone ahead of the 2028 presidential election. Trump would still have the ability to veto legislation that Congress passed, severely limiting Democratic bills' prospects of becoming law.

Must-pass legislation would be slightly easier to get through Congress if Democrats controlled both chambers. But deals would still need to be brokered with the White House, and the Senate's 60-vote filibuster threshold could still prove a hurdle that requires bipartisanship. Whichever party gains Senate control is highly unlikely to have at least 60 seats.

Democratic lawmakers in both the House and Senate would jump at the chance to lead investigations of the White House and business if given the opportunity.

In either a divided Congress or unified Democratic Congress, analysts expect Trump's use of executive orders will increase due to congressional gridlock.

There is a possibility Republicans maintain control of both chambers of Congress. In that case, Trump's remaining legislative agenda could continue to be enacted, and the status quo would be maintained.

Democrats would still hold some leverage, largely due to the Senate's 60-vote filibuster threshold, but would be limited in investigating the White House and business.

#113. Pfizer hikes full-year outlook on new product growth, taps company veteran as new CFO

Pfizer on Tuesday reported fiscal third-quarter results that topped estimates and hiked its full-year outlook, as demand for its newly launched and acquired products grows.

Pfizer also Tuesday announced that Cecile Guegan, who has held various finance leadership roles at the company over the last two decades, stepped in as chief financial officer this month. She had served as interim CFO since August, when Pfizer's former CFO, Dave Denton, stepped down to take the same position at Nike.

The company is now expecting full-year revenue to total $62 billion to $64 billion, which compares with a previous outlook of $60.5 billion to $62.5 billion. The low end of that sales range would be roughly flat or down slightly compared with 2025 revenue of $62.6 billion. Still, Pfizer cited its "strong year-to-date performance" as a driver for the guidance hike.

In an exclusive interview with CNBC, Pfizer CEO Albert Bourla said the company's Covid product revenue was "very low" this quarter and year, but called it "positive news for us."

"Covid revenues was an overhang on the performance of our stock because of the perception that it is volatile and it's material to us. Actually, we've proven that we can make it irrelevant," Bourla said. "If anything, only an upside because, in the lowest possible Covid performance, we are beating, and we are raising by $1.5 billion because of the overperformance of the other products."

Pfizer reaffirmed its Covid product outlook of roughly $4 billion for the year, expecting the majority of Covid shot sales in the fourth quarter during vaccination season, Guegan said during the call.

The pharmaceutical giant now expects full-year adjusted profit of between $2.95 and $3.05 per share. That compares with previous guidance of between $2.80 and $3 per share.

That includes a one-time $650 million charge, or a negative impact of 10 cents per share, recorded in the third quarter tied to the company's licensing and collaboration agreement with Chinese cancer drugmaker Innovent Biologics.

Pfizer is working on a comeback story after its massive pandemic-era success waned. As demand for Covid products drops and generic rivals threaten its biggest moneymakers, Bourla is looking to jump-start growth through strategic acquisitions, fresh cancer drugs and a big bet on weight loss treatments through its acquisition of Metsera.

"In addition to the revenues that they generate now, and they will continue generating so that we can overcome the patent, the loss of patent exclusivity, we are bringing pipeline that will perform even better in terms of growth," Bourla told CNBC, referring to Pfizer's launched and acquired products.

Pfizer in August expanded its multiyear cost-cutting efforts to help offset sharp declines in Covid product sales, and is now targeting roughly $9.7 billion in net savings through 2029.

Here's what the company reported for the third quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Pfizer reported third-quarter revenue of $16.58 billion, relatively flat from the same period a year ago. The company cited an operational decrease of $57 million, primarily due to lower Covid revenues, and a $15 million negative impact from foreign exchange.

That was offset by strong sales of key products like its blood thinner Eliquis and targeted cancer drug Padcev, which jumped and beat expectations.

Eliquis in particular raked in $2.52 billion in sales, up 25%. Analysts were expecting revenue of $2.40 billion, according to StreetAccount. Padcev posted $720 million in revenue for the period, which compares with the $658 million that analysts were expecting.

Sales of Pfizer's newly launched and acquired products contributed $3.8 billion, growing 32% operationally, Guegan said during the earnings call. That includes Padcev, its respiratory syncytial virus vaccine Abrysvo, its pneumococcal shot Prevnar 20 and oral lung cancer drug Lorbrena, among several other products.

'We continue to invest behind this group of products to mitigate [loss of exclusivity] headwinds over the next several years," Guegan said. "In addition, this quarter's performance demonstrates clear progress against our objective of returning to growth toward the end of the decade."

Pfizer's Covid products also topped estimates for the quarter despite seeing steep sales declines. The company's Covid vaccine generated $808 million in sales, which compares with the $770.4 million that analysts were expecting, StreetAccount estimates show. Meanwhile, Pfizer's antiviral pill, Paxlovid, booked $449 million in sales, coming in above the $394.1 million that analysts were expecting.

The company booked net income of $3.24 billion, or 56 cents per share, for the period. That compares with net income of $3.54 billion, or 62 cents per share, during the third quarter of 2025.

Excluding certain items, including restructuring charges and costs associated with intangible assets, Pfizer posted earnings per share of 87 cents for the quarter.

#109. Disney plans several thousand layoffs, asks some managers to cut costs by up to 20%

Disney is planning to cut several thousand jobs, people familiar with the matter told CNBC.

Some managers at the entertainment giant have been asked to reduce costs by up to 20%, according to the people, who asked not to be named because they were not authorized to speak publicly.

It marks the latest round of layoffs since CEO Josh D'Amaro took the reins earlier this year. The update comes after separate plans in April to cut as many as 1,000 employees, predominantly in the marketing department, and in September to cut about 300 employees, many in human resources and technology divisions.

Bloomberg was first to report the latest round of layoffs.

Company executives warned of the possibility of more reductions in its August earnings report.

"We remain highly focused on reducing costs across the enterprise to create incremental capacity to invest for growth and are evaluating a variety of levers," D'Amaro and CFO Hugh Johnston wrote in an Aug. 5 letter to shareholders. "We are mid-stream in this work and will provide future updates on our progress."

Disney introduced plans in August for an early retirement program for some employees.

"This is one of several actions we're taking to reshape our organization, including involuntary staff reductions that have already begun in some areas and will continue into next year," Chief People Officer Sonia Coleman wrote in a letter to employees on Aug. 24.

Disney had about 231,000 employees as of the close of its fiscal year 2025, which ended in September of that year, according to the company's annual financial report.

Disney is not the only entertainment company looking to reduce costs. Netflix said in a memo last month that it would lay off roughly 6% of its workforce. Skydance, the newly combined company of Paramount and Warner Bros. Discovery, is also expected to cut staff in an effort to realize $6 billion in synergies over three years.

Disney will report its fiscal fourth-quarter earnings on Nov. 17.

#108. Trump DOJ sends target letters to Democrats in Alaska Senate criminal probe: Reports

The Trump administration's months-long campaign to challenge how states conduct elections and pushback against those efforts came to a head on Election Day on Tuesday.

Those efforts have included lawsuits, an attempt to implement a new U.S. Postal Service rule for mail-in ballots this year, threatening letters to state election officials and a plan to increase the number of poll monitors.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm elections

The Department of Justice has sent out more than 20 letters, some to Democrats and other people identifying them as potential targets of a criminal investigation related to the Alaska Senate race, and to other recipients telling them to preserve records for that probe, a source familiar with the matter told CNBC.

The New York Times earlier Tuesday reported that prominent election lawyer Marc Elias was among those sent target letters in the investigation of how a candidate with the same name as incumbent Republican Sen. Dan Sullivan ended up on the ballot of the Alaska race.

Elias has represented Mary Peltola, the Democratic nominee in the Alaska election. In addition to him, at least one official at the Democratic Senatorial Campaign Committee was sent a target letter, The Times reported.

"The department is investigating possible donor fraud and conspiracy charges," according to The Times, citing a person familiar with the investigation.

Receiving a target letter does not mean a person has been charged with a crime, and does not necessarily mean that they will be charged.

In a statement, Elias said, "No one should be surprised that President Trump's Department of Justice is targeting me and my law firm on Election Day."

President Donald Trump, during an interview earlier Tuesday on Fox News' "Fox and Friends," blasted Elias.

"You had the Democrat Party headed up by Elias, their lawyer, who was a bad guy, very bad guy — should go to jail, in my opinion, " Trump said. "But you had the Democrat Party look all over Alaska for somebody named Sullivan."

A federal judge on Sunday dismissed the DOJ lawsuit that sought to delay certification of Alaska's Senate election if incumbent Sen. Sullivan loses by a margin that could be attributable to voter confusion from having another Dan Sullivan on the ballot. The DOJ is appealing that ruling.

That case, and the reported letters to Elias and other Democrats, underscore the likelihood that the Trump administration's election-targeting efforts will continue beyond Election Day.

Democratic lawmakers, attorneys general and advocacy groups are poised for battle if the administration tries to interfere with voting sites or challenge ballot counts, particularly in the states that could determine whether Trump's fellow Republicans lose their narrow Senate majority.

Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., on Sunday night convened the entire Senate Democratic Caucus for a briefing by him and Elias about what a Democratic aide said was the "current landscape of election threats from Trump and his allies." The aide spoke on condition of anonymity to discuss the private briefing.

"We've been, you know, blocking and tackling and protecting and defending our elections for months and through the last few weeks of the voting window, and it'll all culminate tomorrow," California Attorney General Rob Bonta told CNBC on Monday evening. "And each of Trump's attempts has failed."

"I'm not sure what he has up his sleeves," Bonta said of Trump.

Bonta said he and other California officials, along with the 23 other Democratic state attorneys general for months have been preparing for Election Day and potential actions by the Trump administration "to disenfranchise and suppress votes and voters."

"We game-planned, and ... war-gamed and tabletop- and scenario planned all the possibilities," Bonta said.

Bonta said that if extreme events happened, California officials would quickly seek relief from the court system.

He noted, "We have laws in place right now where it is a criminal offense" to remove ballots from the custody and control of an election official.

On Monday, multiple federal courts dealt with urgent motions in election-related lawsuits and appeals by and against the Trump administration.

The DOJ on Monday asked the Supreme Court to block California from counting about 20,000 mail-in ballots from voters whom the DOJ suggests might not be U.S. citizens. Hours later, the Supreme Court rejected that request without any noted dissents.

In U.S. District Court in Washington, a DOJ lawyer told Judge Reggie Walton on Monday that the department expected to deploy 900 people to monitor polling places at sites in all 50 states.

"In 2024, there were about 100 monitors," the lawyer, Kathryn Barragan, said.

The DOJ on Tuesday said it was deploying approximately 1,000 election monitors and observers across the country. The areas it is focusing on are primarily in urban areas that vote Democratic.

Barragan's statement came at a hearing where Walton was considering an emergency request by the NAACP to bar the DOJ from using election monitors who are federal law enforcement personnel.

John Cusick, assistant counsel at the Legal Defense Fund, a civil rights group, said the administration has used the DOJ, Department of Homeland Security and other federal agencies to target election procedures this election cycle for three main reasons.

"First and foremost, it's an attempt to intimidate voters and election officials," Cusick said in an interview.

"The second component is confusion" about election rules, he said. "And the third, the unfortunate part, is trying to lay the groundwork for challenging the post-election counting and certification of ballots."

He said there has been a "constant barrage" by the administration to target election workers and election officials with unlawful actions.

"That has created such a chilling effect," Cusick said. "There's no doubt that the threats are threats to our democracy."

The DOJ, which did not respond to a request for comment by CNBC for this article, has previously defended its election-related efforts.

"Voting by noncitizens and other ineligible voters is a federal crime, and the department will enforce the law whether a case involves one illegal ballot or hundreds," the DOJ's rapid response account on X said on Oct. 12.

Attorney General Todd Blanche, when asked on Oct. 22 if there was any scenario in which the DOJ would try to block states from certifying their election results, conceded that was possible.

"I mean, is there any scenario?" Blanche told reporters in Virginia. "Yes, of course. There's scenarios that we could all come up with, hypothetically, you know, some doomsday scenario, for example."

"It is not DOJ's intention to, and it's never our intention to improperly interfere with elections and the state confirmation process or certification process," the attorney general said.

"But, you know, to say that there's no circumstances that we would ever do that? Of course I'm not going to say that."

Cusick and other experts note the president and executive branch of the federal government have no official role in administering elections, which are handled by the states as designated under the Constitution.

In her ruling Sunday dismissing the DOJ's Alaska Senate lawsuit, District Court Judge Sharon Gleason said the Trump administration was making requests that involve "the time, place, and manner of Alaska's election, which are the responsibilities of the State, not the executive branch of the federal government."

On Monday, a bipartisan group of former election officials in a filing told the 9th Circuit U.S. Court of Appeals that "increased federal pressure and threats on election officials ... are part of a pattern by the administration of attempting to assert authority over elections and questioning the integrity of state and local officials."

The filing came in a case where the DOJ is appealing an order issued Friday by a federal judge in California barring the Trump administration "from carrying out a series of efforts to actually or attempting to intimidate, threaten, or coerce election workers who aid others in exercising their right to vote."

The judge cited threats by the DOJ to prosecute state and local election officials if they process and count ballots using procedures that are legally required.

Bonta, California's attorney general, said he expected Trump and the DOJ To continue targeting states over their election procedures through the next cycle in 2028, when voters will choose a new president.

"We can't be naive about it. We have to expect it to get worse, prepared for it to get worse. I don't want to be alarmist," Bonta said. "I am a realist though, and I see the way he thinks and acts and what he's done, and maybe we'll be prepared for the worst, and it'll never happen."

"But we can't not be prepared for it. And I expect him to escalate as he gets more desperate."

#104. JetBlue raises bag fees for third time in a year as fuel costs bite

JetBlue Airways has raised the price of checking a bag for many customers for the third time in less than a year as high fuel costs continue to eat into the industry's profits.

Checking a first bag for travelers booked in economy will now be $50, up from $45, if purchased more than 24 hours before departure during a nonpeak travel period. If it's paid within 24 hours of departure, the fee will be $60. During a peak period, such as during the holidays or the summer, it will be $55 if purchased more than 24 hours before departure, up from $49, and $70 for within 24 hours of departure.

There are exceptions to the checked bag fees, such as for customers who hold a co-branded JetBlue credit card or who hold elite frequent flyer status.

"We're adjusting checked bag fees to help offset rising fuel and operating costs," JetBlue said in a statement. "This helps us keep our base fares competitive while giving customers the choice to pay for a checked bag only if they need one."

The price of jet fuel, airlines' biggest cost after labor, has roughly doubled this year since the start of the Iran war in February, costing airlines billions and driving up the price of tickets. Jet fuel was going for $4.44 a gallon on Tuesday for the U.S. Gulf of Mexico, according to the U.S. Energy Information Administration.

Other airlines often follow when one carrier raises fees. Major U.S. airlines did so earlier this year after JetBlue raised its bag fees.

The changes were reported earlier by the Thrifty Traveler blog.

#100. Google responds to political backlash over removing search from schools that ban AI

Google is playing defense on Tuesday after a political backlash to its new policy surrounding use of artificial intelligence in schools.

In a blog post titled "Designing responsible AI for learning," Google said it's bringing AI into its Classroom offering with a product called Guided Learning. The New York Times published a story saying that the company will turn off search for schools that reject AI entirely.

Following the Times' story, prominent left-leaning politicians, including Sen. Bernie Sanders from Vermont and New York Mayor Zohran Mamdani voiced their outrage on social media.

"Google is trying to use its monopoly power to force AI into our public schools," Mamdani wrote on X. Sanders wrote that "Google is threatening schools," referring to the policy as "extortion."

Google responded later in the day. In a post on X, the company said its new policy was meant to give schools more choice over how students interact with AI. Google said its Gemini chatbot, Gemini Notebook and search are turned off by default, and that "schools are in control," with the ability to turn them as they see fit.

"We are also updating Search for schools to make it better for learning," Google wrote. "Specifically, AI Mode and AI Overviews will offer step-by-step guided learning through problems, so that students don't get quick answers to schoolwork."

The company's post didn't explicitly respond to the claim that access to search requires use of AI. However, Chris Phillips, vice president for education at Google, told the Times that "Search and AI are deeply integrated."

A Google spokesperson referred CNBC to the X post when asked for comment.

Google's broader practices have come under fire in recent years, with the company losing antitrust cases over its search and advertising businesses. Former Federal Trade Commission Chair Lina Khan aggressively pursued Google and other tech giants, including Meta and Amazon.

Khan said in a post on X on Tuesday that Google's new AI for learning policy highlights "the danger of allowing an unregulated monopolist to control an essential service."

It's the latest controversy related to the impact of technology on children and the role leading internet companies are playing in delivering information.

In August, Meta reached a landmark $16.7 billion settlement in its child safety trial after a number of state attorneys general accused the company of misrepresenting the extent of child mental health harms caused by apps like Facebook and Instagram.

The same month, OpenAI launched ChatGPT for Teens after state attorneys general launched an investigation into the company's safety practices, with Florida Attorney General James Uthmeier, a Republican, filing a lawsuit in June.

The nonprofit Common Sense Media recently gave Google's AI overviews an "unacceptable risk" rating. Last month, Sens. Richard Blumenthal, D-Conn., and Josh Hawley, R-Mo., launched an inquiry over Google's push of AI into classrooms.

Google introduced AI overviews as a search function in May 2024.

— CNBC's MacKenzie Sigalos contributed to this report.

#96. Stock market after midterm elections: What S&P 500 history shows

Markets are sweating it out ahead of the midterm elections, but the event historically heralds strong performance for stocks — regardless of whether it's a red or blue wave, split decision, or status quo in Washington.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm elections

The S&P 500 tends to perform well after both midterm and presidential elections. According to a CNBC analysis of S&P 500 returns after elections from 1980 through 2024, the index rose an average of 0.2% after one day and one week, and 1.1% after one month across both presidential and midterm elections.

A year later, the index gained an average of 13.7% after midterms and 13.8% after presidential elections. Returns were positive in 91% of cases. And the S&P 500 was up after all 11 midterms from 1982 through 2022.

Short-term returns were less reliable, particularly after presidential contests, which produced slightly negative average returns after one day and one week.

"There's some anxiousness before these elections," Ryan Detrick, CNBC contributor and chief market strategist at Carson Group, told CNBC. "Once you are through it, it's a big relief and markets tend to do pretty well."

Still, the market usually prefers divided government: one party in charge of the White House and the other holding at least one house of Congress.

Across 10 midterm and five presidential elections that left no single party in charge, the S&P 500 rose an average 15% after one year. One-year returns were positive in 14 out of 15 cases. By comparison, the index gained an average of 11.5% over the year after elections that resulted in single-party control.

"If you have a huge red wave or a big blue wave, markets actually kind of don't like that as much because you have too much spending one way and too much power," Detrick said.

Leaving Democrats in control of Congress with a Republican in the White House — a scenario that may emerge from the elections on Tuesday — was also positive. A CNBC analysis found that after the three midterms (1986, 1990 and 2006) when Democrats controlled both houses and a Republican held the presidency, the S&P 500 averaged a 0.5% decline on the reaction day but was up 3.4% a month later and 10.9% after a year.

Granted, elections may be coincident events to factors that matter far more for market performance.

The S&P 500 plunged 22.1% in the year after the 2000 elections due to the dotcom bust, marking the worst post-election performance since 1980. Conversely, the S&P 500's best post-election year was a 38.3% gain after the 2020 elections, when markets mounted a massive comeback from the COVID-induced selloff.

Investors should bear in mind that Federal Reserve policy and the corporate earnings cycle ultimately matter more than elections, according to Société Générale.

Stocks declined in the aftermath of the 2018 midterms, for instance, when Democrats wrested control of the House during President Donald Trump's first term. More importantly, the Fed tightened monetary policy that year, raising interest rates four times, and the S&P 500 ended the year down 6.2%, though the market was up 11.7% a year after the election.

The big swing factor now is AI and whether declines in tech will continue. As long as the AI wave is still moving and the economy keeps growing, "this bull market is probably still alive and well," Detrick says.

But a Democratic sweep of the House and Senate would almost certainly pressure the AI infrastructure buildout and could lead to more retrenchment.

The bond market is also skittish with yields rising globally and a mini debt crisis in France. And the war with Iran could get worse post-election if President Trump follows through on threats of more strikes or Iran escalates.

"Seasonality isn't destiny, and worries around Iran, AI and France won't mysteriously vanish," said Jim Reid, Deutsche Bank's global head of macro and thematic research, in a note.

If history is any guide, we could see a Santa rally and S&P 500 gains after the midterms. But this is still an AI-driven market, with geopolitics, the Fed, and bond market very much in charge.

— CNBC's Chris Hayes contributed reporting.

#92. McDonald's is about to report earnings. Here's what to expect

McDonald's is expected to report its third-quarter earnings before the bell on Wednesday.

Here's what Wall Street analysts surveyed by LSEG are expecting the company to report:

Last quarter, McDonald's CEO Chris Kempczinski said the chain's U.S. business fell short, hurt by uneven execution of its value offers and complicated launches of new menu items. In response, the company tapped Skye Anderson as its new U.S. president.

#88. Midterm elections could jolt stocks as Democrats look poised to sweep Congress

The stock market was not pricing in a Democratic sweep of Congress heading into Tuesday and therefore the political climate could be a headwind for investors into year-end.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm elections

Short-term moves aside, the typical post-midterm pattern in the months ahead is bullish. But some wonder if the market could buck the positive historical trend this time because the S&P 500 is already up so much this year going into the election.

As far as certain stocks to watch, a big Democratic win on Tuesday night — especially in the Texas governor race — could add fuel to an artificial intelligence stock sell-off on the notion that more data center restrictions would gain momentum nationally. Defense stocks could come under pressure on fears of limits on spending.

Prediction markets calling for a blue sweep are looking to be correct as election results roll in Tuesday evening.

While investors are signaling "begrudging acceptance" to a base-case scenario that there will indeed be a Democratic sweep, that's not how they were positioned, said Tobin Marcus, head of U.S. policy and politics at Wolfe Research. The firm's recent survey data among more than 100 institutional investors suggests they are not strongly positioned for any particular result.

"Investors seem to have a significant dose of skepticism about whether the final outcome is going to be quite as favorable to Democrats as the polling suggests," Marcus told CNBC, noting that the firm shares the view that the "polling is likely overstating Democratic strength."

JPMorgan sees a blue wave knocking the S&P 500 by about a half percent on Wednesday. Overnight futures were pointing to bigger losses than that on Wednesday.

On the other hand, if Republicans end up retaining both chambers of Congress, that could spur a rally, said Sameer Samana, head of global equities and real assets at the Wells Fargo Investment Institute.

Citigroup strategists said Monday that Republicans losing the House but managing to keep the Senate could be "a tailwind for equities into year-end." JPMorgan traders predict up to a 1% pop on Wednesday if Republicans hold on to that chamber. That is looking less likely.

Looking past any short-term move, stocks tend to rally after the midterms have concluded. In fact, the S&P 500 was up one year out after all midterm elections going back to the early 1980s.

But the S&P 500 has already recorded double-digit gains so far in 2026, so some on Wall Street worry that investors are being overly complacent heading into this year's elections.

During midterm election years, the S&P 500 has typically averaged a 14% drawdown at some point in the second half of the year found Jonathan Krinsky, chief market technician at BTIG.

"The reason stocks often perform well in November during midterms is because [the S&P 500] typically is weak INTO the elections. We have the opposite setup this year," Krinsky wrote last week. "Therefore, while consensus is that we see a post midterm rally, we think the opposite is actually more likely."

It's not helping that the broader market has recently come under significant pressure due to turbulence in the artificial intelligence trade, which led the Nasdaq Composite to post its worst day since June on Friday. AI shares led the market lower again on Monday.

A strong showing from Democrats in Tuesday's midterm elections could pressure the trade even more.

At the state level, increased Democratic control is probably a headwind for stocks associated with data centers and technological capital equipment, analysts said.

"It [would solidify] the early kind of cautionary signs in different states around the advancement of AI infrastructure," Rob Rains at Washington Analysis told CNBC.

Goldman Sachs strategists said this week that a Democratic Congress "might limit data center tax incentives, but a national moratorium looks unlikely in any election outcome."

One example would be Texas — a key hub of the data center buildout. A surprise loss there by Republican Gov. Greg Abbott has the "potential to spook" the market in the short term, Wolfe's Marcus said.

A blue wave might pressure defense shares, while boosting healthcare stocks, analysts and investors said.

"A substantial Democratic sweep could limit real spending growth unless a broader agreement increases both defense and health-care funding," said Alex Sagal, global equity analyst at Wells Fargo Investment Institute. However, he noted that the earnings outlook for the defense industry looks "stable to modestly positive."

Wolfe Research's Marcus said that healthcare is going to be "the biggest focus," as Democrats will push for restoring expired Affordable Care Act enhanced premium subsidies or unwinding some of the Medicaid reforms in the One Big Beautiful Bill Act.

"Healthcare stocks have been doing really well lately," Jed Ellerbroek, portfolio manager at Argent Capital Management, said to CNBC. "I think part of that is the anti-AI trade, but I also think part of it is just optimism that Democrats are taking over."

Ellerbroek is keeping his eye on hospital-related stocks such as HCA Healthcare and Tenet Healthcare as well as health insurers like UnitedHealth and Centene.

Financials was the worst S&P 500 sector in early 2026 on the heels of President Donald Trump calling on Congress to pass a law that would cap credit card interest rates at 10%. While the sector has been able to notch gains since, with its year-to-date rise now at around 3%, things could get more unstable for the group under Democratic control.

"Democrats will come in wanting to show tangible progress on the affordability issue, and that means targeting sectors that are front and center in consumer budgets," said Evercore ISI's Matthew Aks. "We know Trump has populist instincts, populist leanings, and could he pick a couple of issues and decide to team up with the Democrats on those issues and target those industries in a populist way? Absolutely."

Given that Democratic control is often associated with increased regulation, while Republican control is thought to promote deregulation, Democrats could be interested in passing credit card rate caps if they come out victorious.

"I think if Democrats have control of one or both chambers, they would try to pass it and see if they could get Trump to either sign it or force him to veto it, and that would let Democrats kind of score a political point," Aks said.

Trump could restart the war against Iran after holding off on any major strikes leading up to the midterms, a move that could upend the overall market and cause energy stocks to outperform. Crude oil prices jumped more than 10% in October, though they gave a lot of that gain back on Monday.

Gasoline prices stood at $4.17 a gallon on average nationwide Sunday, a nearly 40% increase compared with the same period last year, according to AAA.

"If there are escalations, that could obviously put upward pressure on prices just ahead of Thanksgiving," said Patrick De Haan, head of petroleum analysis at Gas Buddy, "which is also when Americans have really staunch traditions in terms of driving to friends and family."

If Trump decides to take forceful action, it could snuff out hope for a typical post-midterm year-end rally.

Evercore ISI said the S&P 500 could retreat by double digits to end the year at 6,800 as oil spikes if the president "moves to kinetic escalation in Iran ahead of seating the new Congress in January" and Democrats take Congress.

— With reporting by Sarah Min, Tobias Burns and Spencer Kimball.
